Hood’s Sarsaparilla (1884). In this
advertising postcard, a woman’s healthy face

introduction


The first edition of _Thinking with Type_ appeared in 2004. Since
then, designers worldwide have used this book to explore the art
and craft of typography. Twenty years later, every last pica of this
trusty tome has been renovated and refreshed. My understanding of design has grown and stretched, and so has this book. Now
in its third edition, _Thinking with Type_ has more pages and more
content. The layouts have more space to breathe, and the text is
more inclusive and accessible.
_Thinking with Type, Third Edition_ features dozens of new and
interesting fonts. You will see classic fonts, weird fonts, Libre
fonts, Google fonts, Adobe fonts, indie fonts, and fonts and design work created by women and people of color. Leading experts
have contributed visual essays about some of the world’s writing
systems. These introductions to various scripts supplement the
book’s main focus—working with Latin typography. The new
edition also explores basic layout principles, such as visual balance and Gestalt grouping, making this book an integral guide
for graphic design.
_Thinking with Type_ highlights common mistakes and how to fix
them. Previous editions used the phrase “type crimes” to poke
fun at self-important type snobs—myself included. Alas, this
mocking phrase makes light of real crimes and inhumane punishments. In the process of writing this new edition, I thought
about removing typographic gaffes altogether. In the end, I decided that explaining errors in a nonjudgmental way helps readers take loving care of typography’s living traditions.
This book welcomes everyone who works or plays with words.
Typography is a tool for reading, writing, and learning—and for
finding joy and revelation. Many thanks to Letterform Archive
for the stunning photographs of historical works; to my teachers,
students, family, and friends for their care and patience; and to
the designers who wrote down the rules long before me.

latin bible (1455). This book
launched the invention of
movable type in Europe.
The book was printed by
Johannes Gutenberg, Johann
Fust, and Peter Schoeffer in
Mainz, Germany. Artisans
added decorative initials by
hand to match the luxurious
appearance of manuscripts.
Reproduced with kind
permission, Letterform
Archive.


+ On the history of printing,
see Michael F. Suarez S.J. and
H. R. Woudhuysen, _The Book: A_
_Global History_ (Oxford: Oxford
University Press, 2013) and Alan
Bartram, _Five Hundred Years of_
_Book Design_ (London: British
Library, 2001).



Writing systems emerge from the body. The first typefaces
copied calligraphy (which means “beautiful handwriting”) and
everyday scripts. But unlike written forms, typefaces are manufactured symbols designed for repetition. The history of type
reflects tensions between hand and machine production, organic
and geometric forms, and the human body and abstract systems.
These tensions still energize type design today.
Movable type, invented by Johannes Gutenberg, revolutionized writing across Europe. Previously, scribes had made books
by hand, a slow and expensive process. In the system of movable
type, letters are cast from a mold and assembled into forms for
printing. After printing the pages, workers sorted and stored
the letters for reuse. Movable type is considered the first form
of mass production.
Movable type proved efficient for printing alphabetic scripts—
such as Latin, Greek, and Cyrillic—which translate spoken
sounds into a few dozen marks. Although Gutenberg printed
his books with metal type, he wanted his products to look handmade. He created variations of many characters to emulate the
dense, dark script known as blackletter or fraktur. He also created ligatures, characters combining two or more letters into a
single mark. Such details made the process of producing books
less efficient but more naturalistic.
The oldest printed book in existence, _Diamond Sutra_, was
created in China in 868 CE. [+ ] It was produced with woodblock
printing, a technique well-suited to the Chinese writing system,
which employs thousands of unique characters. In this process,
artisans trace characters onto the block and carve around them.
These workers did not need to know how to read, which helped
make the technique economical.



thinking with type, third edition



9


Woodblocks were also vital in Korea, where the vast Buddhist
text _Tripitaka_, printed 1236–1251 CE, required more than eighty
thousand blocks. In addition to carving wood, Korean printers
developed metal type in the thirteenth century. Movable type
proved especially suitable for printing the Korean alphabet,
Hangeul (also spelled Hangul), designed by King Sejong in 1443.
Writing systems and techniques for copying them have flourished around the world. [+] Although Gutenberg was unaware of
Asian printing history, his invention built on various precedents
in Europe, including woodblocks, manuscripts, wine presses,
and metal coins stamped with punches.



Diamond Sutra (868 CE).
Printed in China, this book
employed carved wood
blocks printed on loose
sheets of paper, later
assembled into a scroll.
Collection of the British
Library.

**HUMANISM**
**AND THE BODY**


+ On the origins of roman type,
see Gerrit Noordzij, _Letterletter_
(Vancouver: Hartley and Marks,

Francesco Griffo designed
roman and italic types for
Aldus Manutius. Roman
and italic were conceived
as separate typefaces.
This page was printed in
1525. Reproduced with kind
permission, Letterform
Archive.



In fifteenth-century Italy, humanist writers and scholars employed various styles of handwriting. Lettera Antiqua is a classical script with wide, open forms. The preference for Lettera Antiqua was part of the Renaissance (rebirth) of classical Greek and
Roman art, architecture, and scholarship, a movement known
as humanism. Nicolas Jenson, a Frenchman who had learned
to print in Germany, established an influential printing firm in
Venice around 1469. His typefaces merged the Gothic traditions
of France and Germany with the Italian taste for rounder, lighter
forms. He created some of the first roman typefaces. [+]
Many typefaces in use today, including Garamond, Bembo,
Palatino, and Jenson, are named for printers who worked in the
fifteenth and sixteenth centuries. The style of these typefaces is
called humanist. Over time, revivals of historical typefaces have
been designed to work with changing technologies and current
demands for sharpness, uniformity, language support, and more.
Some revivals are based on metal types, punches, or drawings
that still exist; most rely on printed specimens.
Italic letters, introduced in fifteenth-century Italy, were modeled on a more casual handwriting style. While the upright humanist forms appeared in expensive manuscript books, the cursive style thrived in the cheaper writing shops, where it could
be written more quickly. Aldus Manutius, a Venetian printer,
publisher, and scholar, used italic typefaces in his internationally distributed series of small, inexpensive printed books. For
calligraphers, the italic form was cheaper because it saved time,
while for printers, the cursive form saved space. Aldus Manutius
often paired cursive letters with roman capitals; the two styles
were considered fundamentally distinct.
In the sixteenth century, printers began integrating roman
and italic forms into type families with matching weights and
x-heights (the height of the main body of the lowercase letter).
Today, the italic style in many fonts is more than a slanted version of the roman; it incorporates the curves, angles, and narrower proportions associated with cursive forms.

**BIOGRAPHY OF A HUMANIST REVIVAL**


1471 | Nicolas Jenson
Jenson learned to print
in Mainz, the German
birthplace of typography,
before establishing his own
printing press in Venice. The
strokes resemble the path
of a broad-nibbed pen. The
presses and paper of the
era yielded blunt, imperfect
impressions. Reproduced
with kind permission,
Letterform Archive.


1890 | Golden Type
English design reformer
William Morris critiqued the
degradation of factory labor.
His Golden Type rejected
the sparkling, high-contrast
typefaces that dominated
commercial printing in
favor of the solemn density
and soft edges of Jenson’s
printed letters. Reproduced
with kind permission,
Letterform Archive.



1995 | Adobe Jenson
Designed by Robert
Slimbach, Adobe Jenson
emphasizes the ribbonlike
strokes in Jenson’s letters.
The slanted bar of the _e_
extends slightly past the
curve.


2020 | Epica
This superfamily, designed
by Oscar Guerrero Cañizares,
includes numerous weights
in both serif and sans-serif
styles. The strokes feel
closer to the Renaissance
than Adobe Jenson because
they have less contrast.

**ABSTRACTION**


Geofroy Tory wrote in 1529,
“The cross-stroke covers the
man’s organ of generation,
to signify that Modesty and
Chastity are required, before
all else, in those who seek
acquaintance with wellshaped letters.” Reproduced
with kind permission,
Letterform Archive.


The Romain du Roi (King’s
Roman) was designed for
the printing press of Louis
XIV in France in 1695, using a
fine grid. Philippe Grandjean
created a typeface based on
the theoretical drawings.


+ On the search for essential
letterforms since the
Renaissance, see Kate Brideau,
_The Typographic Medium_
(Cambridge, MA: MIT Press,
2022).
++ F. E. Pardoe, _John Baskerville_
_of_ _Birmingham: Letter-Founder_
_and Printer_ (London: Frederick
Muller Limited, 1975), 68.



Renaissance artists derived proportions from idealized human bodies. The French typographer Geofroy Tory published
a treatise in 1529 linking letterforms to human figures. [+ ] A new
approach—distanced from the body—would unfold during the
Enlightenment, an age of scientific and philosophical inquiry.
A committee appointed by Louis XIV in France in 1693 set out
to construct roman letters against a finely meshed grid. Whereas
Tory’s diagrams were produced as woodcuts, the gridded drawings of the Romain du Roi (King’s Roman) used engraving. This
process employs a tool called a graver to incise a copper plate.
The typefaces derived from these diagrams were influenced by
the sharp, linear quality of engraving as well as by the scientific
aspirations of the project.
Louis Barbedor, George Bickham, and other writing masters
taught penmanship to the elite classes and disseminated their
work via engravings, whose swelling, serpentine lines capture the
motion of handwriting. Bickham’s book _The Universal Penman_
(1743) features crisp roman letters—each engraved as a unique
character—as well as flowing scripts.
In England, William Caslon and John Baskerville abandoned
the rigid nib of humanist calligraphy for the flexible steel pen and
the pointed quill of the new writing masters. Baskerville, himself
a master calligrapher, would have admired the thinly sculpted
lines printed in the engraved writing books. Because his typefaces were so sharp, contemporaries accused him of “blinding all the
Readers in the Nation; for the strokes of your letters, being too thin
and narrow, hurt the Eye.” [++] To heighten the detail and contrast of
his typography, Baskerville made his own inks and hot-pressed
his pages after printing.
At the turn of the nineteenth century, Giambattista Bodoni in
Italy and Firmin Didot in France amplified Baskerville’s precise
typographic style. Their typefaces have a strong vertical axis, severe contrast between thick and thin strokes, and crisp, waferlike
serifs. This glittering new style launched an approach to typography unhinged from calligraphy.

**MONSTER FONTS**



By rejecting calligraphy and humanist proportions, Bodoni and
Didot unleashed a strange new world. Features such as serif and
stem, thick and thin strokes, and vertical and horizontal stress
could be freely manipulated. In search of a beauty both rational
and sublime, Bodoni and Didot had created a monster: an abstract approach to letters set free from the body and hand.





Rob Roy Kelly studied the
mechanized design strategies
that generated a spectacular
variety of display letters in
the nineteenth century. The
diagram above shows how
the basic square serif form—
called Egyptian or slab—was
cut, pinched, pulled, and
curled to spawn new species
of ornament. Serifs were
transformed from calligraphic
end strokes into independent
geometric elements that
could be freely adjusted.


+ On decorated types, see Rob
Roy Kelly, _American Wood_
_Type: 1828–1900, Notes on the_
_Evolution of Decorated and_
_Large Letters_ (New York: Da
Capo Press, 1969), Nicolete Gray,
_A History of Lettering_ (Oxford:
Phaidon Press, 1986), and Ruari
McLean, “An Examination of
Egyptians,” in _Texts on Type:_
_Critical Writings on Typography_,
ed. Steven Heller and Philip
B. Meggs (New York: Allworth
Press, 2001), 70–76.



Mass production and mass consumption exploded in the
nineteenth century. The new medium of advertising demanded new styles of typography. Type designers grabbed attention
by embellishing, engorging, stretching, and squeezing the body
parts of letters. Fonts of astonishing height, width, and depth
appeared—expanded, contracted, shadowed, inlined, fattened,
faceted, and floriated. Serifs abandoned their supporting role to
become independent architectural structures. The traditional
stress of Latin letters canted in new directions.
Lead, the material for casting metal type, is too soft to hold
its shape at large sizes under the pressure of the printing press.
Wood type, however, can be printed at gigantic scales. In 1834
the combined pantograph and router revolutionized wood-type
manufacture. The pantograph is a tracing device that, when
linked to a router for carving, allows the designer to make variants of one parent drawing, creating alphabets with different
proportions, weights, and details. [+]

This mechanized design method is divorced from calligraphy.
The search for perfect archetypes grounded in idealized human
figures gave way to a new view of typography as an elastic system of structural features (weight, stress, stem, crossbars, serifs,
angles, curves, ascenders, descenders, and so on). The relationships among letters in a typeface became more important than
the identity of individual characters.



18 HUMANS AND MACHINES


Fat Face is the name given to
the inflated, hyper-bold type
style introduced in the early
nineteenth century. These
designs exaggerated the thick
and thin components seen
in the typographic forms of
Bodoni and Didot.


Condensed typefaces violated
the classical proportions
of lettering. Nineteenthcentury advertisements often
combined fonts of varying
styles and widths. Condensed
type could force a word or
phrase into a small space.


without serifs. Gothic letters command attention with their massive
frontality. Although sans-serif typefaces were later associated with
rational neutrality, their early purpose was to attract attention.


Slab serifs transformed the serif from
a delicate ornament into a load-bearing
structure. The slab style was also called
Egyptian, referring to the enormous scale and
bottom-heavy architecture of the pyramids.
Europeans had an exoticized fascination with
African art, design, and hieroglyphics. This
style first appeared around 1806.

Exploring the dangers of

considered the first work of science
fiction. Like the mad scientist in
Shelley’s novel, commercial type
designers in the nineteenth century
distorted the natural state of
letters, alarming traditionalists.

style drawn with horizontal stress (bottom).
Typographic critics would later call Caslon’s
Italian a “monstrosity.” [+] _Caslon’s Specimen of_
_Printing Types_ (1844). Reproduced with kind
permission, Letterform Archive.

**REFORM AND**
**REVOLUTION**


Edward Johnston based
this 1906 diagram of
essential letterforms on
ancient Roman inscriptions.
While deriding commercial
lettering, Johnston
appreciated medieval
ornament. _Writing &_
_Illuminating & Lettering_ (Sir
Isaac Pitman & Sons, 1932).



Some designers condemned distortions of the alphabet, blaming industrialization for corrupting art, design, and typography. Writing in 1906, Edward Johnston renewed the search for
an ideal alphabet and denounced exaggeration. Inspired by the
nineteenth-century Arts and Crafts Movement, he looked back
to the Renaissance and the Middle Ages for pure, uncorrupted
letterforms. Johnston and other reformers critiqued the commercial mainstream.
Avant-garde artists and poets sought to dissolve the barriers between art and everyday life. They drew experimental alphabets and arranged existing typefaces in new ways. The Italian poet F. T. Marinetti published the “Futurist Manifesto” in
1909. His typographic poems combine different styles and sizes
of type, working against the rigid rectilinear order of letterpress
printing. Futurism exposed the technological grid of letterpress
while pushing beyond it. Dada artists and poets used mass media
tools—from typography to film and photomontage—to attack
conventional life and social institutions.
In the Netherlands, Piet Mondrian divided his paintings with
vertical and horizontal lines that seemed to expand beyond the
limits of the canvas. Theo van Doesburg, Piet Zwart, and other
members of the Dutch De Stijl group applied this idea to design
and typography. Converting the curves and angles of the alphabet into perpendicular units, they forced the letter through the
mesh of the grid. Vilmos Huszár built his hand-drawn logo for
the magazine _De Stijl_ with modular blocks.
Constructivism, launched in the Soviet Union at the end of the
1910s, built on Futurist and Dada typography. El Lissitzky used
printer’s rules and ornaments to divide space vertically and horizontally, foregrounding the mechanical matrix of letterpress.
The page was no longer a fixed, hierarchical window for viewing
content but an open expanse to be mapped and marked with letters, rules, and ornaments. El Lissitzky traveled extensively in
Europe in the 1920s, where he met the German Dada artist Kurt
Schwitters and influenced many avant-garde designers.

REFORM AND
REVOLUTION


+ Christopher Burke, _Paul_
_Renner: The Art of Typography_
(New York: Princeton
Architectural Press, 1998) and
Douglas Thomas, _Never Use_
_Futura_ (New York: Princeton

Futura, designed by Paul
Renner in Germany in 1927,
has sharp A’s and circular
O’s. Renner designed
numerous weights of Futura.
The type family was a tool for
painting the page in shades
of black and gray.



Piet Zwart was influenced by the De Stijl movement and then
by El Lissitzky and Constructivism. In his work for Fortoliet,
a flooring company, Zwart combined existing typefaces with
inventive new letterforms pieced together from the materials
of the type shop. He combined visionary letter design with the
ready-made mechanics of typography.
Herbert Bayer was a student and instructor at the Bauhaus, a
German art school. He used drafting tools to construct letters
with basic geometric forms. Such experiments approached the
alphabet as a modular system of abstract relationships. Typography was becoming a critical tool that reflected on its own making.
Assembled like machines from uniform parts, avant-garde alphabets emulated factory production—yet few were executed as
working typefaces for letterpress printing. In 1927 Paul Renner
created Futura, a commercially viable font family that embodied
the machine dreams of the avant-garde. Futura’s serene, abstract
forms aimed to “dispense with handwritten movement.” Designers
around the world embraced Renner’s font as an ideal match for
modernity. Futura quickly became one of the world’s most popular typefaces. [+]

**TYPE AS**
**PROGRAM**


+ Wim Crouwel, _New_

++Rudy VanderLans and

Responding to the rise of electronic communication, the Dutch
designer Wim Crouwel published designs for a “new alphabet” in
1967, constructed from straight lines. He designed his letters for
optimal display on a video screen (CRT), which renders curves
and angles with horizontal scan lines. Some of his characters are
legible only in the context of the system. [+]


new alphabet | _Wim Crouwel, 1967_


In the mid-1980s, personal computers spread the tools of typography to a broader public. Zuzana Licko’s typefaces exploited the
rough, jaggy grain of early screen displays and dot-matrix printers. She founded Emigre Fonts and _Emigre_ magazine with Rudy
VanderLans, heralding a new digital era. [++]


lo-res | _Zuzana Licko, 1985_


Variable fonts, introduced in 2016, generate endless type styles
from one font file. A core design can generate gradations of
weight, width, and other features. Fraunces, by Phaedra Charles
and Flavia Zimbardi, includes a softness variable, allowing the
terminals of the letters to harden or melt.


fraunces | _Phaedra Charles, Flavia Zimbardi, 2018_


Designers keep exploring the elastic, fantastic potential of digital
media. Emma Hall’s wavy-gravy typeface Ponte Glitch (2019)
romanticizes the fault lines of technology.


Ponte Glitch | _Emma Hall, 2019_ | _Text: Legacy Russell,_ Glitch Feminism _, 2020_



26 HUMANS AND MACHINES


Wim Crouwel published his designs for a
“new alphabet,” consisting of no diagonals
or curves, in 1967. The Foundry (London)
created digital editions of Crouwel’s
typefaces.


thinking with type, third edition



27


**TYPE AS**
**NARRATIVE**


+ Schessa Garbutt, “&&&:
Provoking Type,” _The Black_
_Experience in Design:_
_Identity, Expression, &_
_Reflection_, edited by Anne
H. Berry et al. (New York:
Allworth Press, 2022),
392–400.



In the early 1990s, as digital design tools began supporting the
seamless integration of type, image, and time-based media,
many designers grew dissatisfied with clean, unsullied surfaces.
Instead, they sought to expose letterforms to physical processes.
Barry Deck’s typeface Template Gothic emulates plastic stencils, tools that are both mechanical and manual. Deck designed
Template Gothic while he was a student of Ed Fella, whose experimental posters inspired many type designers in the 1990s.


template gothic | _Barry Deck, 1990_


P. Scott Makela’s typeface Dead History is a mashup of the traditional serif font Centennial and the pop-culture classic VAG
Rounded, created for Volkswagen AG in 1979.


dead history | _P. Scott Makela, 1990_


The Dutch typographers Erik van Blokland and Just van Rossum
are programmers and designers. Their 1990 randomized typeface Beowulf changes each time it is used.


Typefaces by Tré Seals celebrate Black history. His font Bayard
pays tribute to the life of activist Bayard Rustin and the Civil
Rights posters of the 1950s and ’60s. His work is expanding the
White-centered canon of Western typography. [+]


bayard | _Tré Seals, 2018_



28 HUMANS AND MACHINES


**BACK TO WORK**



Throughout the digital type revolution, designers continued to
build functional typefaces designed for the rigors of extended
reading and screen-based media. Licko produced historical revivals alongside her experimental alphabets. Her 1996 typeface
Mrs Eaves, inspired by Baskerville, became one of the most beloved typefaces of its time. In 2009, Mrs Eaves was joined by Mr
Eaves, her sans-serif companion.

working _femme_ **seeks** SIDE **kick**

mrs eaves | _Zuzana Licko, 1996_ mr eaves | _Zuzana Licko, 2009_


Martin Majoor’s Scala, introduced in 1991, offered a crisp renewal of typographic history. Scala’s simply drawn, decisively
geometric serifs looked back to humanist styles from a contemporary point of view. Scala expanded to become a superfamily,
with serif and sans-serif forms in numerous weights and styles.

_Renaissance_ **shapes** & modern **details**

scala | _Martin Majoor, 1991_ scala sans | _Martin Majoor, 1993_


Tobias Frere-Jones designed Gotham in 2000, inspired by letters found at a New York City bus terminal. Gotham became the
typeface of Barack Obama’s 2008 presidential campaign. Typography’s First Family grew to include more than fifty styles.

Rock-solid **CONSTRUCTION**

gotham | ultra | light | bold | _Tobias Frere-Jones, 2000_


Designers choose typefaces by considering the history of type,
the connotations of form, the balance between readability and
surprise, the content and themes at hand, and the designer’s own
desires and interests. No rule book assigns a fixed meaning to every typeface; each designer confronts the library of possibilities
in light of a project’s unique circumstances.



thinking with type, third edition



29


**EVERYONE,**
**EVERYWHERE**



Typefaces can connect communities and protect local traditions.
1.8 billion people live on the Indian subcontinent. They speak
hundreds of languages, including many that are indigenous and
endangered. The November type system, an ongoing project of
Typotheque, supports a dozen Indic scripts; the native scripts
of North America; Thai, Lao, and Khmer in Southeast Asia; and
many more large and small writing systems.

+ David Jonathan Ross,
“Backasswards, Revisited,”
_Amalgam_, Op. 1 (2018): 84–89.


++ Ben Wittner, Sascha

Experimental type designers are challenging the norms of Western typography. Letters drawn with heavy horizontal strokes
look strange to readers of the Latin alphabet. Such typefaces
often are called reverse or inverted contrast, marking them as
deviations from the (Latin) norm. [+] Yet not all writing traditions
share Latin’s strong vertical emphasis. Arabic, Hebrew, and Indic
scripts are typically designed with horizontal stress.


tortellini | _David Jonathan Ross, 2019_


Curiosity and necessity compel designers to learn about different
languages and different ways to read and write. [++] Typo­graphy
can preserve cultures, connect diverse groups, express identity,
and expand collective notions of beauty and history. Delight and
understanding are everywhere to be found.



30 HUMANS AND MACHINES


Alseny Diallo created Jangu Adlam,
an app for teaching ADLaM, in 2020.
The ADLaM alphabet is used for writing
Fulani, a language widely spoken in
West Africa and the African diaspora.
ADLaM was created in the 1980s by
Abdoulaye and Ibrahima Barry, who
grew up in Guinea. Diallo’s app is
typeset in Noto Adlam, designed by
Diane Colier/Monotype, 2016.


Mirna Pierre designed her typeface
View after researching the history of
Black graphic designers and studying
design and fashion popular in the
1970s. She created the typeface for
the AIGA SPOT Mentorship program
in 2022. View’s horizontal stress
and dramatic curves reflect Pierre’s
research and personal journey.


Chris Skillern, a citizen of the
Cherokee Nation, named his typeface
Meli (pronounced may-lee) after his
daughter, Mary. Meli is the Cherokee
name for Mary. Inspired by children’s
books, Meli (2021) includes a brushy,
pseudo-sans display style, a friendly
serif text face, and a text italic. The
font supports the Cherokee syllabary,
designed by Sequoyah in 1821. Meli
aims to appeal to younger readers.

Several guides are added to align letters
that share similar structures. Guides for
nabirah/loop heights are added for letters
above the middle line such as _sin_, _qaf,_ and
_fa_ . Descender guides are added for letters
below the middle line such as _ra_ and _ya._

Chinese characters employ subunits
called strokes. Each stroke is a continuous
writing motion. Strokes are often described
anthropomorphically in terms of bones and
sinews. The strokes cluster around a core
and radiate from a focal point (the center of
gravity). There are eight traditional categories
of strokes, exemplified by the character for
eternity (above).


There are no strict rules governing the alignment
of Chinese and Latin scripts. Chinese characters
are aligned according to the center of gravity,
while Latin characters are aligned along the
baseline. Combining the two requires judgment.
Generally, the placement of Chinese characters
is judged relative to the cap height and x-height
of the Latin typeface and the overall color
(texture) generated by the two scripts.



In type design, however, the typology of strokes
differs slightly, along with variations in how they
are described.


The cap height of the Latin typeface is
typically centered vertically against the height
of the Chinese characters. The descenders
are taken into account optically. Depending
on the typeface, this means that the top of
the x-height may intersect with the Chinese
characters’ center of gravity, as shown above.
Latin letters should never be aligned to the
top or bottom edge of a character.

**KOREAN |** WRITTEN AND DESIGNED BY MOON JUNG JANG AND TAEKYEOM LEE



Korean (Hangeul) typefaces have
two primary styles: Buri and Minburi.
The Buri style has many slight
projections—spurs, knobs, and
dots—because it is derived from
script and calligraphy. Some letter
combinations are designed in two
versions: one with a longer stem/
pillar and the other with a short stem/
pillar. The length is determined based
on which letter it is put together with.





Pillar


Upper
Side Stem

Lower
Side Stem


Terminal







Top



Turn Spur





Bridge


A buri is the bent tip of
a stroke in Hangeul. It is
similar to a serif in Latin
fonts. Minburi means
"without buri," similar to
sans serif.


A bo is a horizontal stem.
It originated from an
essential building element,
the bo or daedeulbo on
the pillar supporting a
roof in traditional Korean
architecture.

kana

katakana



Japanese characters include ideographic
kanji and syllabic kana (katakana and
hiragana). Generally, each kanji character
occupies 90 to 95 percent of the area in
its square body, while a kana character
is slightly smaller, occupying 83 to 88
percent of the body area. The majority of
characters in Japanese texts are hiragana



and katakana. These syllabic characters
need to be designed carefully to achieve
good readability. Hiragana and katakana
characters are expressed through diverse
letterforms, reflecting various typeface
styles and designs, including Mincho, Gothic,
Rounded Gothic, and many others. Typical
Japanese texts also include Latin characters.

**INDIC |** WRITTEN AND DESIGNED BY AKSHITA CHANDRA


Indic scripts, also called Brahmic, descend from the
ancient Indian Brahmi script. Indic writing systems are
used across the Indian subcontinent and in Southeast
Asia and parts of East Asia. Dozens of different scripts
support hundreds of languages. The graphic elements
of a few Indic scripts are shown here.

Height



The size of a typeface is determined by the em square, a vertical
dimension that usually encompasses the tallest ascender and the
longest descender. If the type size is 14 points, the glyphs occupy an em square that is 14 points high. Characters can fill this
entire vertical area or leave a small space buffer. Ascenders and
descenders sometimes extend slightly beyond the em square.

Pixels (px) are fixed units.
One pixel is equal to one
pixel unit on the screen. The
size of the pixel depends on
the screen.


Points (pt) are fixed values.
They are traditionally used
in print but can also be
used on-screen. Inches and
millimeters can also be used.

42 TYPEFACES AND FONTS


Different fonts occupy the vertical canvas of the em square in
different ways. Additionally, software systems and web browsers
create frames or containers with different heights and properties. Since no fixed rule dictates how fonts occupy these spaces,
graphic designers need to account for the quirks of fonts and
software when mixing typefaces from different families. Two
typefaces set at the same point size or pixel size may look larger
or smaller, depending on how they occupy the em square.

depending on how they fill the em square.
Some layout software allows designers to
customize text frames to account for these
differences.

thinking with type, third edition 43


HEIGHT **X-HEIGHT**


The x-height is the size of the main part of a lowercase letter. Two
typefaces set in the same size can have different x-heights. The
size of the x-height affects a typeface’s space efficiency and legibility. Typefaces with big x-heights have larger counters, making
them look airy, open, and bigger overall. Like hemlines and hairstyles, x-heights go in and out of fashion. Big x-heights became
popular in the mid-twentieth century and continue to have a
functional advantage. Typefaces with smaller bodies, while less
efficient, have a lyrical charm.


When the x-height is large
relative to the cap height,
the forms of the lowercase
letters become bigger and
more open.


_(Firmin Didot, 1799)_



Body Size | Typefaces with
small x-heights (Metro and
Brandon Grotesque) have
a more antique flair, while
those with larger x-heights
(Akzidenz-Grotesk and
National) feel more airy and
modern.

Texture Studies | The x-height affects the density
of a text block. Typefaces with a large x-height, such
as Tiempos, appear denser, with compressed space
between the lines, while typefaces with a small
x-height, such as Brandon Grotesque, appear to have
Text from Mary Wollstonecraft more space between the lines. Designers adjust the
Shelley, _Frankenstein_, 1818 size and spacing to achieve the desired texture.


thinking with type, third edition 45


Width



Each letter in a typeface has a horizontal measure, called its set
width. The set width is the body of the letter plus a sliver of space
(called a side bearing) protecting it from other letters. In most
typefaces, each letter has its own width. The characters in a given
typeface will be more or less narrow or wide.
You can change the width of a typeface by fiddling with its
horizontal or vertical scale. This distorts the line weight of the
letters, however, forcing heavy elements to become thin and
thin elements to become thick. To avoid unwanted distortions,
choose a typeface with the proportions you are looking for, such
as condensed, compressed, wide, or extended.



Interstate Black
The letter _w_ has a wide set
width. The letter _i_ has a
narrow set width.


Interstate | _Tobias Frere-Jones, 1993_


× Horizontal & Vertical Scaling ♥

The proportions of the letters have
been distorted to create wider or
narrower letters. Sometimes this
technique forces characters to fill a
given space. Do this only if you intend
to distort the letterforms.

WIDTH **MONOSPACE**

Most Latin typefaces are proportional: each letter has its own
width. In a monospace font, every character occupies the same
amount of horizontal space. By forcing the letter _L_ to fill the same
area as an _M_ or _W,_ monospace fonts create a spotty yet mechanical texture, and they take up more space than proportional typefaces. Monospace text often appears in computer code, engineering drawings, and documents written on mechanical typewriters.
Designers can highlight the DIY aesthetics of monospace fonts
or use them for contrast with more organic type styles.

WIDTH **UNIWIDTH**



+ Lisa Staudinger, “Uniwidth
Typefaces for Interface Design,”
UX Collective, January 27,
2021, uxdesign.cc/uniwidthtypefaces-for-interface-designb6e8078dc0f7.



Different weights in a uniwidth typeface occupy the same horizontal space. Unlike a monospace font, each letter has its own
width. However, that character’s width stays the same across different weights. This feature is useful in charts, tables, and other
data graphics because words won’t get wider when the weight
changes from light to bold. Uniwidth typefaces also work well in
user interfaces because the text in a button or menu can change
weight when activated without breaking the layout. [+] Uniwidth
fonts are also called equal-width, duplexed, or multiplexed.


Noi Grotesk | _Robin Eberwein,_
_Felix Pfäffli_, _2021_
This variable font has uniwidth
characters. Note that the
letterspacing gets tighter as
the letters get bolder.



52 TYPEFACES AND FONTS


Lamm & Kirch (Florian Lamm, Jakob Kirch) designed a
comprehensive visual identity for Urbane Künst Ruhr
in 2018. The identity uses a custom variable font based
on Folio (Konrad Bauer, Walter Baum, 1957).



The letters have the same width in every weight.
Thus the font can change in an animation or in
different versions of a single printed piece without
breaking the layout.



Service Gothic | _Nick Sherman, 2020_ Service Gothic is inspired by signs from
pizzerias and parking lots. Its uniwidth
characteristic makes it useful for animations,
signage, interfaces, and data graphics.


thinking with type, third edition 53


Classifying
Typefaces


+ For a deep dive into type
anatomy and classification,
see Stephen Coles, _The_
_Anatomy of Type: A Graphic_
_Guide to 100 Typefaces_ (New
York: Harper Design, 2012).
Paul McNeil’s book _The Visual_
_History of Type_ avoids type
classifications by taking
a chronological approach
(London: Laurence King
Publishing, 2017).


**HUMANIST**
Also called old style,
humanist typefaces emulate
the first century of printing.
The calligraphic strokes have
moderate contrast and are
drawn with angled stress.


**HUMANIST SANS**
Some sans-serif typefaces
look back to humanist styles
for shapes and proportions.
Note the complex curves,
subtle variations in line
weight, and small, lilting
counter in the letter _a_ .



Nineteenth-century printers grouped typefaces into basic categories, hoping to establish a common language and an artistic heritage for their craft during a period of rapid commercial
growth. Humanist typefaces emulate Renaissance calligraphy
and handwriting. Transitional and modern typefaces are drawn
with sharper details and stronger contrast. [+] Slab serif and
sans-serif styles were used in posters and advertising. Countless styles fall outside these basic categories, such as blackletter,
scripts, and ornamental typefaces.
Type classification is a tool, not a science. Many typefaces
openly defy traditional categories. Today, some font platforms
use familiar adjectives like “rounded,” “luxury,” “funky,” and
“fun” to help anyone find typefaces.



**SLAB**
A slab serif is similar in
weight to the letter’s
main strokes. This style
enabled the creation of
bolder typefaces for use
in advertising or to create
emphasis in text.


**SCRIPTS AND MORE**
Magasin is a script face
designed by Laura Meseguer.
Its contrast and vertical
stress are similar to the
typefaces of Bodoni and
Didot, which were also
inspired by penmanship.



**TRANSITIONAL**
Typefaces such as Caslon
and Baskerville have sharper
serifs and a more vertical
axis than humanist letters.
Improved presses, paper,
and ink helped deliver more
precise forms.


**GROTESQUE SANS**
Most sans-serif typefaces
created in the early
nineteenth century have
proportions and shapes
similar to the transitional
and modern serif faces that
were dominant at the time.



**MODERN**
The typefaces created by
Bodoni and Didot at the
beginning of the nineteenth
century have thin, straight
serifs, a vertical axis, and
strong contrast from thick
to thin.


**GEOMETRIC SANS**
In the twentieth century,
designs like Futura brought
a new geometric sensibility
to typeface design. These
typefaces expressed a more
deliberate personality than
the anonymous grotesques.



54 TYPEFACES AND FONTS


humanist


transitional


modern


slab


humanist sans


grotesque sans

**Akzidenz-**
**Grotesk**


geometric sans



Introduced in 1964, Sabon was designed by the German-born designer Jan
Tschichold. It was based on the sixteenth-century typefaces of Robert
Granjon, who was a contemporary of Claude Garamond in France. The
crisp, _ribbonlike forms_ of Sabon are modeled on the path of a pen.


When John Baskerville introduced his typefaces in England in the 1750s, their
_sharp forms and high contrast_ were considered shocking. Across the Atlantic,
Baskerville was championed by Benjamin Franklin, a skilled printer, designer,
inventor, and politician.


The typefaces created by Firmin Didot between 1799 and 1811 are severe
and abstract. The Didot foundry also engaged in _printing, publishing, and_
_paper manufacturing_ in France. Adrian Frutiger designed Didot LT for
Linotype in 1991.


The typeface Clarendon was introduced by Thorowgood and Co. in
London in 1845, designed by Robert Beasley. Clarendon has wide
proportions and slightly bracketed serifs. This version is by URW
Type Foundry.


The gently sloping bowl of the lowercase a reveals the humanist sensibility of
Freight Sans. Designed by Joshua Darden, Freight Sans belongs to the Freight
superfamily (2005), which includes _serif and sans-serif typefaces_ in an extensive
range of weights and optical sizes.


Introduced in Germany by the Berthold type foundry in 1898, AkzidenzGrotesk quickly became a _popular sans-serif typeface_ . After acquiring other
type foundries, Berthold added fonts such as Royal Grotesk, by Ferdinand
Theinhardt, to the Akzidenz family.


The O’s of Futura, designed by Paul Renner in Germany in 1927, are nearly perfect
circles, while the strokes of the A, M, N, and W _converge in sharp points_ . Futura
Now, a variable font with clarified weights and sizes, was designed by Steve
Matteson, Terrance Weinzierl, and Juan Villanueva for Monotype in 2020.



thinking with type, third edition 55


CLASSIFYING
TYPEFACES



Grouping typefaces into categories can help designers choose
from the abundance of fonts. These categories are loose, not absolute. While some designs are easy to describe, others adopt
elements from multiple categories. Over time, designers have
revised, removed, recombined, and inflated familiar features to
reinterpret the history of typography and create new designs.

Choosing
Typefaces



A publication, brand, or website can function beautifully with a
single family of typefaces. Superfamilies like Freight include numerous weights and styles, including serif, sans serif, and optical
sizes. Many projects combine fonts from two or more families,
using each one to serve different functions or bring different attitudes to the project. Consider the functional requirements of
a project and the aesthetic qualities you are looking for. Pick a
primary typeface or family first, and add other styles as needed.

58 TYPEFACES AND FONTS


**TIPS FOR CHOOSING TYPEFACES**


→ Ask what will readers do with your content (read, wander,
scan, buy, sell, customize). What functions do you need
(space efficiency, language support, multiple styles)?

→ Ask what voice or attitude do you want to convey to readers
(confidence, candor, mystery, authority, intimacy).

→ Test different typefaces with sample content from your
project. What feels right?

→ Read typeface reviews and subscribe to design newsletters.
Inspect the code on websites that you like.

CHOOSING
TYPEFACES


+ On the philosophy of type
pairing, see Bethany Heck,
“The Value of Multi-Typeface
Design,” _Medium_, May 2, 2016,
medium.com/s/about-face/thevalue-of-multi-typeface-designccd67227b0ee.


Many font pairings have
some differences (such
as serif/sans serif) and
some similarities (such
as narrow proportions).



width, proportion
x-height
shape of a, g, r, y
length of ascenders & descenders
humanist shapes
stress, verticality
terminals (such as ball terminals)
serif shape (wedge, slab, bracket)
stroke (monoline, calligraphic)
geometry (square, round)
spacing (loose, tight)
authorship (who designed it?)
historical origins
language support
number of styles and weights



**PAIRING TYPEFACES**


Why—and how—do designers combine different typefaces in a
single layout or design system? A second or third typeface may
serve a specific function, such as providing backup for a snappy
headline. A simple method is to use a superfamily for one-stop
shopping. Fear not, however, the perils of a more varied mix.
Look for similarities as well as differences when pairing typefaces. The difference factor is obvious. Why else would you mix
typefaces if you weren’t craving contrasts (such as more curves,
more sparkle, or more peace and quiet)? In addition to their
differences, however, font pairs should share common features,
such as narrow or wide proportions, similar shapes in the lowercase _a_ or _g_, or mutual cultural or historical heritage. [+]

Why are wine and cheese served together? First, there is a delightful contrast between fatty, luscious cheese and sharp, acidic wine. Cheese and wine have similarities, too: they are funky
fermented goods created through a process of controlled decay.
The chart below provides some categories for describing similarities and differences among typefaces. Objective features include who designed the typeface or how wide its characters are.
Subjective qualities include memories, trends, or sensory impact. Subjectivity is okay! Mixing typefaces is a personal process.


**OBJECTIVE QUALITIES** **SUBJECTIVE QUALITIES**

queerness (nonconforming parts and angles)
animal/vegetable/mineral
cool name

CHOOSING
TYPEFACES


Type 1 fonts consist of two
files: a screen font and a
printer font. This early font
format has become obsolete.


TrueType (TT) was created by
Apple and Microsoft to use
with their operating systems.
TrueType fonts are easier
to install than Type 1 fonts
because they consist of a
single font file.



OpenType (OTF), developed by
Adobe, works across multiple
operating systems. Each file
holds up to 65,000 characters,
supporting multiple styles
and character variations in
a single font file. OpenType
fonts are often labeled “Pro.”
Features include the ability
to automatically adjust
the position of hyphens,
parentheses, and brackets for
letters set in all caps.



**FONT FORMATS AND LICENSES**


Fonts are distributed by big companies, small collectives, and individual designers. Fonts are sold, rented, bundled, borrowed,
stolen, and given away (with and without their creators’ permission). Font formats help typefaces work with different printers,
browsers, operating systems, and devices. For web typography,
fonts can be hosted on platforms like Adobe and Google or your
own server, enabling instant delivery to users’ browsers.
Digital fonts are easy to copy and alter. When you purchase
a font, you accept an end-user license agreement (EULA) that
limits how you can use it. Intellectual property law in the United
States protects fonts as software. It is a violation of most EULAs
to copy and share fonts or to open the font file, alter the characters, and save the font under a new name. EULAs vary from
foundry to foundry. Follow EULAs carefully to avoid legal disputes and ensure that typeface designers are justly compensated.



Web Open Font Format
(WOFF) was developed by
Mozilla and members of the
type design community.
This compressed font
format loads quickly and
can include metadata about
font licensing. Formatted
for the web, WOFF fonts
benefit typeface designers,
foundries, web designers, and
end users.



Variable Font (VF) is a
font format that enables a
continuous range of variants
to exist in a single font file.
Although weight is the most
commonly used variable,
designers have employed
other standard and custom
variables to create fonts
that reduce file clutter and
prioritize flexible use. See
Variable Fonts, page 84.

Type Families



One type family can perform many tasks. With a few weights and
styles, designers can display complex bodies of text with coordinated headings, subheads, captions, and other elements. Over
time, type designers have developed some common terms for
naming type styles, while type families have grown bigger and
more complex, supporting the demands of branding, packaging,
signage, and editorial design. One big type family might have
more styles than a designer could sensibly use in one project.
When working with a type family, experiment with its diverse
bodies and personalities. Consider what purpose the different
font choices might have in your project.


Univers | _Adrian Frutiger, 1957_
The Swiss typeface was issued in twenty-one
versions, in five weights and five widths.
Whereas some type families grow over time,
Univers was conceived as a total system from
its inception.

**A BASIC TYPE FAMILY**


The core or norm of a typical type family is the _regular_ weight.

edita regular | _Pilar Cano, 2009_


Some typefaces include a slightly heavier weight called _book_ .

edita book


_**Bold**_ **weights, employed for emphasis, strive to stand out.**

edita bold


The different weights of a type family share such characteristics
as basic proportion, stress, contrast, serif shape, cap height, and
x-height. A functional type family for setting bodies of text on
page or screen should include _italic_ styles for each weight or style.
_Italic_ letters serve to provide emphasis without adding weight to
a line of text. Especially among serif faces, the _italic_ style often
employs shapes and strokes that are distinct from its upright
counterpart. Note the structural difference between the roman
and italic _a_ in the typeface used here, Edita. **Bold** weights produce
more **dramatic emphasis** than _italic_ styles. Some typefaces also
include small caps, which are capital letters whose height is
similar to the x-height. Small caps integrate with lowercase
letters in situations where full-size CAPITALS disturb the peace.

weight keep it from appearing too ethereal.

**SUPERFAMILIES**


Type families that include both serif and sans-serif styles are
called superfamilies. Jeremy Tankard’s Trilogy is a single family
that harmonizes the mismatched rhythm of nineteenth-century
commercial advertisements. The Adelle family consists of dozens of fonts in multiple weights and widths, while Scala provides
a more bookish range of options. Superfamilies offer graphic designers the convenience of one-stop shopping.

Rotis | _Otl Aicher, 1989_
Aicher challenged binary thinking with this
superfamily that includes steps between
serif and sans serif. Sometime in the
twentieth century, typography became
divided into the either/or categories
of serif and sans. Perhaps Aicher was
ahead of his time. Rotis has never been
especially popular, but it represents bold
thinking about family structure.

The earliest alphabets were drawn in one height. There was no
need for an extra set of letters because the purpose of an alphabet
is to translate sounds into speech. Latin letters carved into stone
embodied the power of the Roman Empire. Carved capitals were
the basis for the capital letters printed in books in fifteenthcentury Venice. Incorporated into modern typefaces, roman
capitals retain some of their imposing presence. Capitalizing
a name or a title marks its importance and bestows formality.
From uppercase to upper class, having the upper hand signals
dominance and hierarchy. [+] Modern communication media make
typographic authority available to everyone.

+ On the philosophy of italics

Although false italics are

such as _s_ and _o_ .



♥ true italic × false italic ♥ roman


upright italic backslant



Italics add movement to static blocks of text. The first italic
typefaces were based on handwriting. Today, italics are used for
lyrical details and urgent headlines but rarely for long bodies of
text. Centuries of habit make italics seem out of place for extended reading. In sans-serif typefaces, the italic style is sometimes
called oblique; these slanted romans follow the upright form.
English-language typesetting italicizes the titles of books and
films (such as _The Color Purple_ ) and phrases written in a different language (such as _fermé la bouche_ ). These distinctions can be
achieved through other means, including underlining and letterspacing, which preserve the vertical direction of roman text.
Layout software and web browsers automatically slant the roman font when a true italic isn’t available, yielding a typographic faux pas appalling to purists. The rules, however, are open to
change. [+] Dissident forms include upright italics (who refuse to
bow their heads) and back slant italics (who lean the wrong way).

Lining numerals resemble capital letters. Oldstyle numerals (also
called text or nonlining numerals) look like lowercase letters,
with ascenders and descenders. Tabular numerals have uniform
widths—ideal for tables and charts. Proportional numerals have
varying widths and/or spacing. Lining and nonlining numerals
can be designed with tabular and/or proportional spacing. Some
type families offer all four styles.
Indian mathematicians developed the decimal numeral system in the first century CE. Arab mathematicians adopted the
system in the ninth century CE. [+] Hindu-Arabic numerals later
traveled to Europe. The Italian scholar Fibonacci, who visited
Algeria, popularized this number system in Europe in his book
_Liber Abaci_, written for merchants and traders in 1202 CE.

thinking with type, third edition 75


Punctuation



Marks of punctuation, such as commas, periods, semicolons, and
parentheses, divide text into sentences and phrases. These silent
glyphs can convey the essence of a typeface. Helvetica’s comma
is a chunky square mounted to a jaunty curve, while Bodoni’s
comma is a voluptuous, thin-stemmed blossom.
Punctuation is governed by grammatical and typographic
rules. Designers apply these rules when crafting headlines and
making text corrections. Punctuation rules and marks vary from
language to language. When in doubt, check your text with
grammar software and/or a native speaker.

**HATCH MARKS AND TYPOGRAPHER’S QUOTES**

5'2" eyes of blue

Hatch marks (Prime marks) indicate dimensions in feet
(single prime) and inches (double prime).

It’s a long day’s journey.

Apostrophes indicate a contraction, such as “it’s” instead
of “it is.” They also indicate a possessive noun.

He said, “I do.”

Typographer’s quotes are also called curly quotes, smart
quotes, or open and closing quotes. They enclose spoken
dialogue or the title of an essay, poem, song, or episode of a
TV series. The opening quotation mark is different from the
closing mark.


**MARKING QUOTATIONS IN VARIOUS LANGUAGES**



“Hello.”american english
‘Hello.’british english
«Olá.»portuguese
»Hej.«danish
„Zdravo.“slovenian



Punctuation follows different conventions
worldwide, even among languages written
in the Latin alphabet. American English
uses double curly quotes, while British
English employs single ones.


Pointy symbols called guillemets or
chevrons are used in Portuguese, French,
Italian, German, and Danish, among other
languages. Guillemets sit on the baseline.
They point out in most languages, but in
Germany and Denmark, they point in.


Slovenian, Slovak, and Polish are written
with curly quotes; the first quotation mark
sits on the baseline.


read more | “Use of Quotation Marks in Different

Union, op.europa.eu/en/web/eu-vocabularies/

thinking with type, third edition 77


PUNCTUATION Working with punctuation is not just about following rules
and avoiding mistakes. Punctuation can be expressive, playful,
decorative, and functional. It can also help promote genderinclusive language, which rejects universal masculine forms and
binary gender distinctions. Inclusive language matters because

The German gender star ( _Gendersternchen_ ) diminishes the
gender binary by using an asterisk to signal the inclusion of all
genders. Introduced in 2013, the gender star is gaining acceptance—while also meeting resistance. [++]



**THE GENDER STAR**


driver, male driver, female drivers, all genders

Gender star | An asterisk
appears between the stem of the
word and the feminine ending.
Thus the German word _Fahrer_
(male, driver, singular) becomes
the inclusive _Fahrer*innen_ (all
drivers).


78 TYPEFACES AND FONTS



Typography and feminisms (opposite).
Professor Stefanie Schwarz and her students
at Bauhaus-Universität Weimar investigated
feminist typography in 2020 and 2021.
They analyzed the gender star and similar
characters used for the same purpose.
Their design proposals include new symbol
concepts, larger star characters, and closer
integration between the star and surrounding
letterforms. In German, the word _feminist_
indicates a male feminist, while _feministin_ is
a female feminist. The new form, _feminist*in_,
includes all genders.


thinking with type, third edition 79


Optical Sizes



A type family with optical sizes includes different fonts designed
for use at different sizes of output. Styles designed for use at large
sizes are called headline, display, or titling fonts. Typically, these
styles have more delicate details than those labeled text, caption,
or micro. Smaller fonts have features such as sturdier strokes,
taller x-heights, and larger counters. Variable fonts with optical
sizes appear automatically, based on context.

thinking with type, third edition 83


Variable
Fonts



A variable font is a single font file that supports multiple weights
and/or styles. Eliminating font file clutter makes variable fonts
especially valuable on the web. OpenType supports five standard
or registered axes, an established feature that can be adjusted by
users. The registered axes are italic, optical size, slant, weight,
and width. In CSS (Cascading Style Sheets)these axes correspond to registered properties (ital, opsz, slnt, wght, and wdth).
Weight is the most commonly included property.
The named instances in a variable font are equivalent to the
styles in a traditional type family (such as light, regular, and
bold), or the weights and widths in CSS. Designers can pick endless points between these preset instances. Tiny increments can
be useful for designing animations or logos.

small variations in weight.
Underware’s type specimen
shows a potential use for
choosing special weights.

VARIABLE FONTS In addition to using registered axes, type designers can invent
their own custom axes, such as gravity (GRVT), or devise precise
parametric axes, such as x-height (YTLC), to adjust a single facet
of a letterform’s anatomy. Most font axes have a range (such as
100–900), while a few toggle on and off (0 or 1). Optical size
variations can be triggered automatically by page design software or CSS; designers can choose to turn this behavior off.



bracketing | `BR` | 0 `BR` | 1 cookies toggle | `TC` | 1


86 TYPEFACES AND FONTS



Buffalo Gals | _Tom Rickner, 1992_
This early variable font includes two axes
that toggle on and off. The Bracketing
axis displays two serif styles. The Cookies
Toggle adds decorative holes.


Climate Crisis | _Tuomas Jääskeläinen, 2021_
The Nordic newspaper _Helsingin Sanomat_
created this font with the custom axis Year. The
heaviest weight represents the extent of Arctic
sea ice in 1979. The lightest weight represents
the sea ice coverage expected in 2050.


**CUSTOM AXES**


James T. Edmondson, the founder of
Ohno Type Company, is the creator of
Cheee, a soft, blobby font whose styles
include Stinkhead, Sticky, and Gnat.
Cheee Variable has a yeast axis, which
inflates the forms, and a gravity axis,
which makes them bottom heavy.


**PARAMETRIC FONTS**


Parametric fonts allow typeface users to
independently manipulate various features
of the letterforms. David Berlow’s Amstelvar
is an experimental design commissioned by
Google Fonts. Its custom axes include cap
height, x-height, and the height and depth
of ascenders and descenders. Potential uses
of parametric fonts include matching the
proportions of two different typefaces. The
letterforms shown here have been pushed to
the extreme end of each axis.

Ornaments



Not all typographic elements represent language. For centuries, ornaments have been designed to print on the page in the
same medium as text. Letterpress printers assemble decorative
elements one by one to build larger forms and patterns on the
page. Decorative rules and straight lines serve to frame and divide content. In the nineteenth century, printers provided their
customers with vast collections of ready-made illustrations that
could easily be mixed with text. Ornaments designed as digital
fonts can be typed, scaled, and output like any typeface.


Typographic ornaments
(Fry and Steele, 1794).

permission, Letterform
Archive.

a single ornament from the typeface
Whirligigs to create an ethereal veil of
ink. Whirligigs are modular units that
fit together to create an infinite variety
of patterns.

ORNAMENTS



In the nineteenth century, polychrome alphabets were used for
signs, packaging, and advertising. Today, layered typefaces and
color fonts allow designers to create many complex ornamental
designs with a single type family, employing outlines, inlines,
shadows, alternate characters, and more.
Ecuadorian designer Vanessa Zúñiga Tinizaray analyzes the
morphological structure of Indigenous designs to create modular systems. The letterforms and ornaments in her typeface
Nunka Anent fit together to create patterns, images, and words.

Many graphic designers enjoy learning the basics of typeface design. Training courses and software have helped this specialized
profession become more accessible. Producing a complete typeface is an enormous task. However, for people with a knack for
drawing letterforms, the process is hugely rewarding. [+]

The first step in designing a typeface is defining a concept.
Will the letters be serif, sans serif, or something else? Will they
be modular or organic? Will they be constructed geometrically
or based on writing and calligraphy? Will they be used for display
or text? Will they draw on historical sources?
The next step is creating drawings. Some designers start with
pencil drawings before working digitally, while others build their
letterforms directly with software. Begin with a few core letters,
such as o, u, h, and n. Build curves, lines, and shapes that will reappear throughout the font. The letters in a typeface often share
attributes such as x-height, line weight, and stress.
Control the spacing of the typeface by adding blank areas next
to each character and creating kerning pairs that set the distance
between particular characters.



94 TYPEFACES AND FONTS


mercury | _Hoefler & Frere-Jones, 1999_
The typeface Mercury has chunky serifs
and sturdy upright strokes. Designer
Jonathan Hoefler marked notes on the
proof above, commenting on everything
from the width or weight of a letter to
the size and shape of a serif. Many proofs
are made during the design process.
In a digital typeface, each letterform
consists of a series of curves and lines
controlled by points. Different weights
and widths can be made automatically by
interpolating between extremes such as
light and heavy or narrow and wide. The
designer then adjusts each outline.

beginner



Make a prototype for a bitmap typeface by designing letters with
a grid of squares or a grid of dots. Substitute the curves and diagonals of traditional letterforms with these rigid elements. Avoid
making detailed “staircases,” which are just curves and diagonals
in disguise. This exercise looks back to the geometric typefaces
made by avant-garde designers in the 1910s and ’20s. The project
also speaks to digital technologies, such as cash register receipts
and LED signs.

102


readers, writers,
and users


In typography, text is defined as a continuous sequence of words,
distinct from shorter headlines or captions. The main mass of
text in a document is called the body. Also known as running
text, this content can flow from one page, column, or box to another. Text can be viewed as a thing—a solid, sturdy block—or
a shapeless fluid poured into the containers of a page or screen.
Text can be solid or liquid, body or blood.
Designers generally treat a body of text consistently from page
to page. Body text is a coherent substance distributed across a
document. Contemporary designers and writers produce content for use in many contexts, from pages of print to an array of
screen conditions and digital devices, each posing its own limits
and opportunities.
Designers help readers navigate the flood of words by building
bridges, shortcuts, and alternate routes. From a simple indent
(marking the entrance to a new idea) to a highlighted link (announcing a jump to a new location), typography helps readers
traverse bodies of content. Headings and tags enable searching
and indexing by humans and machines. A user could be looking
for specific data (a price or movie time) or absorbing a complex
body of information (a Shakespeare play or a Wikipedia article).
Visual cues and structural labels make text usable and accessible.
Good typography enhances readability, helping people consume more text for longer periods of time. Good typography
can also help people read _less_, equipping them to jump quickly in
and out of content.

climbing up the side of the
page to replace a piece of
faulty text. The corrected

104 READERS, WRITERS, AND USERS


**ERRORS AND**
**OWNERSHIP**


+ Marshall McLuhan,
_The Gutenberg Galaxy_
(Toronto: University of
Toronto Press, 1962).


++ Eric Schrijver, _Copy This_
_Book: An Artists’ Guide_
_to Copyright_ (Rotterdam:
Onomotopee, 2019).
+++ Byung-Chul Han,
_Shanzhai: Deconstruction_
_in Chinese_ (Cambridge, MA:
MIT Press, 2017).



In European literary traditions, typography sealed the notion of
a text as a complete, original work—a stable body with an essential form. Manuscripts were riddled with errors. Copies were
copied from copies, each with its own glitches and gaps. Scribes
devised inventive ways to insert missing lines into manuscripts
to repair these expensive, laboriously crafted objects.
Printing with movable type was the first system of mass production. Marshall McLuhan wrote, “Typography tended to alter
language from a means of perception and exploration to a portable
commodity.” [+] As in other forms of mass production, the price of
manufacturing a run of printed books is high. Setting type, correcting mistakes, and operating a press require costly equipment
and skilled labor. The price of each unit drops, however, as the
number of prints increases. The printing system allows editors
and authors to correct a work as it passes from manuscript to
typeset pages. Text supplied to a printer is often called copy. Test
prints, traditionally called proofs, are made before a book, poster, package, or other piece is printed. Proofreaders ensure the
faithfulness of printed matter to the author or client’s original.
European copyright laws, written in the early eighteenth century, protect authors’ rights to their creative work. [++] In Chinese
traditions, authorship could be shared between a poet and the
connoisseurs invited to add their own commentary. [+++] Today,
some people promote the liberty of data and ideas while others
hope to protect—sometimes indefinitely—their control of intellectual property.
Even texts that have passed through the castle gates of print
can change. Each edition of a book (such as this one) represents
one fossil record of a text. The meaning changes when the work
is translated, quoted, revised, interpreted, or taught. ~~Electronic~~
~~redlining and a procession of digital versions have replaced hard~~
~~copy.~~ Online texts can be downloaded and reformatted. A classic
typographic page emphasizes the completeness and closure of
a work. Alternative design strategies acknowledge change and
collaboration.

Technology, Schizo­
phrenia, Electric Speech
(University of Nebraska
Press, 1989). This book,
designed by Richard
Eckersley with author Avital
Ronell, explores writing
as technology. The layouts
underscore the argument
of the text. This spread
embraces typographic rivers,
gaps that connect vertically
through the page. Rivers
violate the unified texture
of traditional typography.
Photograph: Dan Meyers.


106 READERS, WRITERS, AND USERS


**SPACING**


+ Walter Ong, _Orality_
_and Literacy: The_
_Technologizing of the Word_
(London and New York:
Methuen, 1981), 121.


++ Jacques Derrida, _Of_
_Grammatology,_ trans.
Gayatri Chakravorty Spivak
(Baltimore: Johns Hopkins
University Press, 1976), 39.


+++ See Khajag Apelian
and Wael Morcos’s essay
in this volume, “Arabic
Typography,” 172–75.



Typography is as much an act of spacing as an act of marking. In
letterpress printing, blank slugs of lead and slivers of copper create gaps between words or letters. Strips of metal (called leading)
divide text into horizontal lines; wider blocks (called furniture)
define the margins of the page. Voids become things.
Scholar Walter Ong has described how typography turned
words, spaces, and texts into objects in space. Printing with type
“situates words in space more relentlessly than writing ever did.
Writing moves words from the sound world to the world of visual
space, but print locks words into position in this space. Control of
position is everything in print.” [+] Typography turned text into a
material object with fixed dimensions and known locations.
Spoken language has no audible gaps. Spacing became crucial,
however, to writing with the Greek and Latin alphabets. Letters
translate the sounds of speech into strings of characters. Word
spaces were introduced after the invention of the Greek alphabet
to break those long strings of characters into units. Tryreadingalineoftextwithoutspacingtoseehowimportantithasbecome.
The French philosopher Jacques Derrida, who created the theory of deconstruction, tried to untangle assumptions embedded
in language and society. Although the Latin alphabet is a system
for representing spoken sounds, the system fails without blank
spaces and silent marks: “That a speech supposedly alive can lend
itself to spacing in its own writing is what relates to its own death.” [++]
Spacing and punctuation are seen but not heard. The Latin alphabet overpowered the spoken word as phonetic writing became an increasingly visual, silent medium.
Although readers of Latin typography take word spacing for
granted, some writing systems, including Chinese and Japanese,
employ no word spacing or limited word spacing. The silent distinctions between uppercase and lowercase and roman and italic
are absent from most scripts. In Arabic, tatweel or madd is the
practice of elongating a character to justify a line or provide emphasis. [+++] The character itself becomes a form of spacing.



thinking with type, third edition



107


**LINEARITY**


+ Roland Barthes, “From
Work to Text,” _Image/_
_Music/Text_, trans. Stephen
Heath (New York: Hill
and Wang, 1977), 159.



In his essay “From Work to Text,” the critic Roland Barthes presented two opposing models of writing: the closed, fixed “work”
and the open, unstable “text.” The work is a tidy, neatly packaged
object, proofread and copyrighted, made complete by the art of
printing. The work asserts the role of the author as the keeper of
a text’s meaning and purpose. Western literary traditions revere
the author as a solitary, godlike creator.
The text, in contrast to the work, is leaky and open. It migrates
across a dispersed web of standard plots and received ideas. It
thrives on cultural references and the idioms of other writers and
makers. Barthes pictured the text as “woven entirely with citations,
references, echoes, cultural languages (what language is not?), antecedent and contemporary, which cut across and through in a vast
stereophony....The metaphor of the Text is that of the network.” [+ ]

Writing in the 1960s and ’70s, Barthes anticipated the Internet
as a decentralized web of connections.
The single block of text on a traditional printed page is framed
and supported by the navigational features of the book, from
page numbers, headings, and footnotes to the table of contents,
abstract, appendix, and index. These tools mark locations and
allow readers to come and go at will. Such devices—invented by
publishers and printers—took advantage of the new structure
of the typographic book. A printed work is a fixed sequence of
pages, a body lodged in a grid of coordinates.
The marginal devices of print are attacks on linearity, providing means of entrance and escape from the one-way stream of
discourse. Whereas talking flows in a single direction, writing
occupies both space and time. Typography taps that spatial dimension and liberates readers from strict linearity.
Digital tools support linear and nonlinear authorship. Word
processing programs treat documents as a linear stream, while
page layout software creates draggable boxes that can sit anywhere in space. Databases allow layouts to assemble on the fly
in response to user searches. Page titles, keywords, and alt tags
invite data to be searched, indexed, and recalled.



108 READERS, WRITERS, AND USERS


++ Lev Manovich, _The_
_Language of New Media_
(Cambridge, MA: MIT Press,
2002).


On the Origin of Species:
The Preservation of
Favoured Traces (2009).
This experimental edition
of Darwin’s famous treatise
was compiled by John van
Wyhe and designed by Ben
Fry. It highlights changes
made by Darwin across
six editions of the book.
Darwin continually added
and revised content.



Databases are the structure behind electronic games, digital
publications, and product catalogs. These genres create an information space rather than a fixed linear sequence. Media critic
Lev Manovich has described language itself as a kind of database,
an archive of elements from which people assemble the utterances of speech. [++] Many design projects emphasize spatial layout
over linear sequence. The system or template has become more
important than the individual instance. Designers often work
across architecture, typography, film, wayfinding, and other media. By dramatizing the spatial quality of a project, designers can
help readers navigate complex documents and environments.
An increasingly complex use of space marks the history of typography. In the digital age, characters are summoned by keystrokes, touch screens, and voice commands, not gathered from
heavy drawers filled with metal. Space has become more liquid
than concrete, and typography has evolved from a stable body of
objects to a flexible system of attributes.



thinking with type, third edition



109


110 READERS, WRITERS, AND USERS


**BIRTH OF**
**THE USER**


+ Barthes, “From Work to
Text,” 172.


++ Barthes. “Death of the
Author” (1968), _Image/Music/_
_Text_ (New York: Hill and Wang,
1977), 142–48.
+++ Beatrice Warde, “The
Crystal Goblet, or Why
Printing Should Be Invisible,”
in _Graphic Design Theory:_
_Readings from the Field_,
edited by Helen Armstrong
(New York: Princeton
Architectural Press, 2009),
39–43.


Cranbrook Design:
The New Discourse
(Rizzoli, 1990). Under the
direction of Katherine
and Michael McCoy, the
graduate program in graphic
and industrial design at
Cranbrook Academy of Art
was a leading center
for experimental design from
the 1970s through the early
1990s. McCoy developed
a model of “typography
as discourse,” in which
the designer and reader
actively interpret a text.
Book designed by Katherine
McCoy, P. Scott Makela, and
Mary Lou Kroh. Photograph:
Dan Meyers.



Replacing the closed, perfect work with an open web of references empowers readers to create meaning. The reader “plays” the
text as a musician plays an instrument. Authors cannot dictate
the outcome of their own creations. Barthes wrote, “The text itself plays (like a door, like a machine with ‘play’) and the reader
plays twice over, playing the Text as one plays a game, looking for a
practice which reproduces it.” [+] Like a vocalist singing a song, the
reader performs the written word.
Graphic designers embraced the idea of the readerly text in
the 1980s and early ’90s, using layered typography and interlocking grids to explore Barthes’s theory of the “death of the author.” [++] They rejected the idea of typography as a crystal goblet
for content, a metaphor invented by type critic Beatrice Warde
in 1930. [+++] Readerly typography is not a self-effacing vessel. It is
an active process of interpretation.
Redefining typography as “discourse,” designer Katherine
McCoy imploded the dichotomy between seeing and reading.
Pictures can be read (analyzed, decoded, and taken apart), and
words can be seen (viewed as shapes, lines, and textures). Valuing
ambiguity and complexity, McCoy challenged readers to produce their own meaning and championed the designer as author.
Another model surfaced at the end of the 1990s, borrowed
not from literary theory but from human-computer interaction
and usability design. The dominant subject of the digital age is
neither the reader nor author but the user, a figure conceived
as a bundle of needs—cognitive, physical, and emotional. Like
a patient or child, the user must be protected, scrutinized, and
controlled, submitted to research and testing.
How texts are used outweighs what they mean. Someone
clicked here to get there. Someone who bought this also bought
that. Interactive media give users the appearance of control and
autonomy. In the background, search engines, social networks,
and AI algorithms gather data about people. A text is a game to
be played, as the user responds to signals from the system. We
play the text, but it also plays us.



thinking with type, third edition



111


BIRTH OF
THE USER


+ On screen readability,
see John D. Gould et al.,
“Reading from CRT Displays
Can Be as Fast as Reading
from Paper,” _Human_
_Factors_ 29, no. 5 (1987):
497–517.


++ On the restless user,
see Jakob Nielsen,
_Designing Web Usability_
(Indianapolis: New
Riders, 2000).



Print and digital media support different user needs and preferences. Studies dating back to the 1980s proved that crisp black
text on a white background can be read as efficiently from a screen
as from a printed page. [+] Yet efficiency alone fails to account for
the tactile pleasures of books and paper. Likewise, printed books
can’t match the accessibility and convenience of the infinite libraries crammed inside handheld devices. Each medium has its
own limits and affordances.
The experience of reading on-screen can feel restless and unsettled. Users may feel trapped in a state of rapid searching rather than slow, readerly absorption, and they may feel distracted
by false leads and competing offers. [++] Online tools for banishing
digital clutter help make space for enjoying long-form reading. Unbroken pages of print also demand design intervention.
Chunking text into smaller parts within a clearly marked structure makes long texts more accessible.
In an era of information overload, a person can still process
only one message at one time. Given the fierce competition for
people’s attention, users have a chance to shape the information
economy by choosing where to look. Designers can help everyone make satisfying choices, creating accessible invitations to
see, read, and think.
Typography is an interface to the alphabet. User theory favors
normative solutions over experimental layouts and interfaces,
pushing design into the background. In everyday experience,
readers ignore the typographic interface and focus on content
and information. Sometimes, however, the interface should be
allowed to fail. By drawing attention to its own forms, typography can help users question assumptions, enjoy the sensory
dimension of media, and engage in collective authorship.



112 READERS, WRITERS, AND USERS

by Louis Quatorze for display at Platform-L.
