thinking with type, third edition 115


Aligning Columns



Centered text is symmetrical,
like the facade of a classical building.



Justified text has even edges along
both sides of the column. Justified text
has been the norm in Latin typo­graphy
since Gutenberg. In metal typesetting,
printers can justify each line by hand,
using small metal spacers to alter the
gaps between words and letters and
thus make all the lines the same length.
Hyphenation breaks words that don’t fit
at the end of a line. Digital typesetting
performs this labor automatically. Justified type makes efficient use of space.
It also creates a crisp shape on the page.


**JUSTIFIED**


Justified columns have crisp edges. Its space
efficiency makes justified text the norm for
newspapers and books. Hyphenation, flexible
word spacing and letterspacing, and longer
line lengths help fix the gaps that occur
when text is forced to fill an even line length.
Justified text is also called “quad left and
right” and “blocked type.”



This style often appears on
invitations, title pages, certificates,



and gravestones.
A centered column can be



dramatically uneven.
Centered lines should be broken to



emphasize key phrases
(such as the name of a bride



or the date of a wedding)
or to allow a new thought



to begin on its own line.
Breaking lines in this manner



is called
breaking for sense.



**CENTERED**


Centered text is formal and classical. The
text typically breaks into phrases and creates
fluid, organic shapes. Centering is often the
simplest and most intuitive way to place
a typographic element, from book covers
to interface buttons. Used without care,
centered text can look static and mournful,
like a tombstone.



116 COLUMNS, LINES, AND SPACING


**FLUSH LEFT/RAG RIGHT**


Flush-left columns have even spacing and an
organic shape. The rough right edge should
appear natural and relaxed. Sometimes
designers will use line breaks or hyphens to
create a more attractive rag. This setting is
also called “unjustified” or “rag right.”



**FLUSH RIGHT/RAG LEFT**


In Latin typography, flush-right text (also
called “rag left”) can offer a welcome
departure from the familiar. Because flushright text is unusual, it can annoy cautious
readers—and the editors who love them.
Periods, commas, and other punctuation
marks can weaken the hard right edge, so try
pushing them into the margin.

Line Length


+ Opinions vary about
readable line lengths. Emil
Ruder recommends 50 to 60
characters, John Kane suggests
35 to 65, and Robert Bringhurst
prefers 45 to 75. We chose a
permissive range (35 to 75). See
Emil Ruder, _Typography_ (New
York: Hastings House, 1981),
38; John Kane, _A Type Primer_
(London: Laurence King, 2002),
88, and Robert Bringhurst, _The_
_Elements of Typographic Style_
(Vancouver: Hartley and Marks,
1992, 1997), 26.


×

Too long | Wide lines
change the shape of each
paragraph, making them
look shallow and starved.


♥

Just right | With shorter
line lengths, the paragraphs
get taller, creating blocks
with welcoming proportions.
You will soon get a feel for
columns that run too wide
or too narrow. Creating a
beautiful column of text
requires adjusting the
type size, line length, line
spacing, and other settings
in concert with each other.


edita | _Pilar Cano, 2009_



When composing a text column, choose a line length (also called

a measure) that works well with your chosen type size, typeface,
and column alignment. A loose recommendation is to create
lines consisting of 35 to 75 characters. [+] This guidance allows
freedom to create different column shapes—skinny, squarish,
or wide—and to design single-column or multicolumn layouts.
On a page or screen, line length contributes to the readability
of text. The eye moves in short movements (called saccades) to
see and process characters. When lines are too long, readers may
struggle to follow one line and find their way to the next. When
lines are too short, readers may get tired of jumping from line to
line. These neurosensory considerations aside, excessively long
or choppy texts can look intimidating and unpleasant.


Once on board the plane, Richard assumed his rightful place in the first-class cabin. Softly
lit recliners were festooned with clean blankets. Heavily branded courtesy kits were stuffed
with eye masks, earplugs, foot-covers, hand cream, and other prophylactics against inflight reality.
Meanwhile, Jane trudged back through the zones of diminishing status. Just beyond the
business-class cabin, bright yellow headrest covers announced “Economy Plus” in 70-pt
Franklin Gothic. Here, sixty-nine extra dollars offered passengers two inches of leg room.

Once on board the plane, Richard assumed his rightful
place in the first-class cabin. Softly lit recliners were festooned with clean blankets. Heavily branded courtesy
kits were stuffed with eye masks, earplugs, foot covers,
hand cream, and other prophylactics against reality.
Jane trudged back through the plane’s zones of diminishing status. Just beyond the business-class cabin,
bright yellow headrest covers announced “Economy
Plus” in 70-pt Franklin Gothic. Here, sixty-nine extra dollars offered passengers two inches of leg room.
Those inches had been stolen, of course, from Economy Minus, where there were no headrest covers and
where Jane’s seat, 95E, sat sandwiched between D and
F. _E_ is for envy.

**LINE LENGTH ON-SCREEN**


For a website or app, line lengths can be
measured in ems, which are the width of the
height of the currently defined type size. In
a 12-pt typeface, one em is 12 pts wide. Line
lengths of 20 to 35 ems will yield 45 to 75
characters per line. See Adam Wathan and
Steve Schoger, www.refactoringui.com/.

thinking with type, third edition 125


Paragraphs



Humans speak in sentences (a set of words expressing a complete
thought) but not in paragraphs (a passage of writing devoted to
a theme or topic). Paragraphs are literary objects. Designed to
divide masses of content into more appetizing portions, they can
be marked with indents, line spaces, line breaks, symbols, and
other visual cues.
Paragraph indents have been common in European print culture since the seventeenth century. Adding space before or after
paragraphs (paragraph spacing) is a standard device in modern
publishing and correspondence. On the web, a paragraph is a semantic unit (contained by the <p> tag) that is typically displayed
on-screen with space included before and after it.
A typical paragraph indent is one em space (or quad), a fixed
unit the width of the letter’s cap height. Deeper indents can be
created with tabs. Avoid indenting the first line of a body of text
or the first line after a headline. An indent signals a break or separation, and a break isn’t needed when the text has just begun.



The table is covered with a table cloth which itself is protected

by a plastic table cloth. Drapes and double drapes are at the

windows. We have carpets, slipcovers, coasters, wain­scoting,

lampshades. Each trinket sits on a doily, each flower in its pot,

and each pot in its saucer.

Everything is protected and surrounded. Even in the gar **­**

den, each cluster is encircled with wire netting, each path is

outlined by bricks, mosaics, or flagstones.

This could be analyzed as an anxious sequestration, as an

obsessional symbolism: the obsession of the cottage owner

and small capitalist not only to possess, but to underline what

he possesses two or three times. There, as other places, the

unconscious speaks in the redundancy of signs, in their conno
tations and overworking.


_— Jean Baudrillard, 1969_

A line with just one or two words at the end of a paragraph, page,

or column of text disrupts the reading flow. You can fix these
short lines or bad breaks by adjusting nearby spacing, hyphenation, or line breaks—or, when appropriate, rewriting the text. In
English, short lines are often called widows and orphans. When
used as epithets, these terms are sexist and insensitive. Neutral
descriptions like “short line” or “bad break” are respectful and
easy to understand.
Is a short line or an awkward page break always unforgivable?
Designers and editors decide whether and how to fix such problems. Depending on the overall layout, preserving a uniform
page or column height may be preferable to breaking the text
where paragraphs naturally end. An occasional short or stranded
line is inevitable in digital formats, where layout is automated
and readers can change the typeface or type size.

**BREAKING PAGES AND COLUMNS**







The burden of matrimony lies heavy on many a
woman who never takes the world into her confidence, and who bears in absolute silence what she
has not the power to cast from her. Perhaps her
husband has been a man of note, a man of learning,
of elevated station, a political or a philanthropic
power. She alone knew the fretfulness, the petty
tyranny, the miserable smallness at home of the
man of large repute whom his generation conspired
to honour. When he died the press wrote his eulogy
and his elegy, but his widow sang softly in her own
heart a pæan to the great King of Freedom, and
whispered a sigh of unutterable relief.
To such a woman widowhood has no sentimental
regrets. She has come into possession of the goods
for which perhaps she sold herself; she is young
enough to enjoy the present and to project a future;
she has the free choice of a maid and the free action
of a matron, as no other woman has. She may be
courted and she need not be chaperoned, nor yet
forced to accept. Experience has mellowed and enriched her yet her loss has not permanently roughened nor embittered her.



Then the sense of relief gladdens, while the sense
of propriety subdues, her; and the delicate mixture
of outside melancholy, tempered with internal
warmth, is wonderfully enticing.
Winter does not last for ever; and a life-long
mourning is a wearisome prospect. So she goes
through her degrees in accurate order, and comes
out at the end radiant. For when the faint shadows
cast by the era of mitigated grief fade away,she is
the freest woman alive. The widow is as thoroughly
emancipated from the conventional bonds which
confine the free action of an unmarried woman as
she is from those which fetter the wife; and only she
herself knows what she has lost and gained.
She bore her yoke well while it pressed on her.
It galled her but she did not wince; only when it
was removed, did she become fully conscious of
how great had been the burden, from her sense
of infinite relief through her freedom. The world
never knew that she had passed under the harrow;
probably therefore it wonders at her cheerfulness,
with the dear departed scarce two years dead; and
some say how sweetly resigned she is, and others
how unfeeling. She is neither. She is simply free

judge the cure worse than the disease,
preferring a fixed column length.

a paragraph can feel jarring. This
situation is often called a widow, a term
considered demeaning.

SHORT LINES



Some languages identify short lines with terms that are even
harsher than “widow” and “orphan.” In Sweden, short lines are
called horungar _._ Like the German word Hurenkind _,_ the Swedish
word means “bastard,” as in “child of a whore.” Designers Brita
Lindvall Leitmann and Alexandra Falagara prefer the Swedish
word busungen (cheeky young rascals). Their publication de
signs (shown here) use short lines to enhance the flow of reading.
Busungen are an “homage to all illegitimate children and their
unmarried mothers.” Leitmann and Falagara helped reform
proofreading lingo in Sweden, where the accepted term is now
ensamrad (single lines).



Syster, 2014 (above). Designed by Bastion
(Brita Lindvall Leitmann and Alexandra
Falagara).

Kerning


**ZERO KERNING**


Here, the kerning has
been set to zero. Note the
awkward spaces between
_Ta_ and _Tw_ .


**METRIC KERNING**


Typically, metric kerning is
the default setting. Metric
kerning uses the spacing
that was intended by the
type designer. Metric
kerning usually looks good,
especially at small sizes.


**OPTICAL KERNING**


Optical kerning is executed
automatically by the page
layout program. This setting
overrides the font’s built-in
kerning. Optical kerning
assesses the shapes of all
characters and adjusts the
spacing automatically.



Adjusting the space between two letters is called kerning. The
characters of the Latin alphabet emerged over time; they were
never designed with fixed spacing in mind. Handwritten letters
naturally flow together but typesetting requires adjustment.
Gaps can occur, for example, around letters with angled and
open forms ( _W_, _Y_, _V_, _T_ ). In digital fonts, the space between
letter pairs is controlled by a kerning table created by the type
designer, which specifies spaces between letter combinations.
A carefully crafted typeface requires little or no extra kerning,
especially in small sizes. Designers often tighten the kerning of
headlines, where gaps become more apparent to the eye.



×



With zero kerning, we can draw a vertical
line between any two letters. The lack of
kerning creates the appearance of a gap.


space between letter pairs
such as _Ta_ and _Tw_ .


Optical kerning has tightened the spacing of
_ke_ and _wo_ as well as _Ta_ and _Tw_ . The kerning
doesn’t look bad, but it’s not what the type
designer intended.

132 COLUMNS, LINES, AND SPACING


**KERNING HEADLINES**


It’s rarely necessary to kern small sizes
of text. (If you find yourself kerning 6-pt
Garamond, it's time to rethink your
priorities.) Headlines, however, often
benefit from tighter spacing, including
manually adjusting individual letter
pairs or using optical kerning to tighten
the spacing overall. Letter pairs that
often need kerning therapy include
_LA_, _To_, _Tr_, _Ta_, _Tu_, _Te_, _Ty_, _Wa_, _WA_, _We_,
_Wo_, _Ya_, and _Yo_ . Some designers use
metric kerning for text sizes and optical
kerning for headlines.

Manually adjusting this letter pair
improves the spacing (bottom).


×


♥


Automatic adjustment
The _Tr_ combination looks too loose
with metric kerning (top). Optical
kerning fixes the problem automatically
(bottom).

Adjusting the spaces across a group of letters is called tracking.
Use tracking to style a heading or create a lighter or denser overall text block. Adjusting the spacing of a single word or phrase
is called letterspacing. Large display type often benefits from
tighter spacing because as letters get bigger, the spaces around
them grow, too.

**FLUSH-LEFT TEXT**



Letters do love one another. However, owing to
their anatomical differences, some letters struggle to achieve intimacy. Consider the letter _V_,
for example, whose seductive valley makes its
limbs stretch out above its base. In contrast, _L_
solidly holds its ground yet harbors a certain
emptiness above the waist. Capital letters, being
square and conservative, prefer to keep some
distance from their neighbors.

calluna | _Jos Buivenga,_

Positive or negative tracking can be applied to bodies of text for
either aesthetic or functional reasons. Aesthetically, tighter or
looser spacing can create a denser or more open texture. Functionally, negative tracking can squeeze more content into a given
area and create more uniform word spacing. Whether deployed
for artistic or practical aims, tracking should be subtle enough to
go unnoticed. Custom tracking overrides the preferences of the
typeface designer, so proceed with care and intention.



136 COLUMNS, LINES, AND SPACING


**TRACKING JUSTIFIED TEXT**


To achieve the uniform line lengths
required for justified text, page
layout software can adjust the spaces
between words and letters. Designers
can specify the maximum, minimum,
and ideal spaces in the Justification
window. The software will first adjust
word spaces and then adjust letter
spaces. If the maximum or minimum
settings can’t be achieved, then a
word will be forced onto the next line,
causing big gaps in the text.

Typographic design
is the best ordering
and correct choice of
type sizes, according
to their place within
the logical structure
of the text.

Line spacing is the distance from one baseline to the next. This
measurement is also called leading (rhymes with _bedding_ ). Leading refers to the blank strips of lead inserted between lines of
metal type. In digital design, line height includes the space above
and below a line of text, measured from between two lines. In
print, the default setting is 120% of the font size (10-pt type is set
with 12 pts of line spacing). On-screen, the typical line height is
1.5 em or 150% of the font size.
Use default spacing as a starting point. Longer lines of text
can be easier to read with a little extra line spacing. Headlines set
with tighter line heights are punchier and more coherent. Typefaces with long descenders and big x-heights may crave more
breathing space. Use your judgment and explore the creative
possibilities of vertical spacing.

It’s fun to apply dramatic
line spacing to inscriptions,
invitations, or block quotes.
For longer passages of text,

distracts and tires the eye.

rows of words become
floating elements rather

9/9 (solid)
Here, 9-pt text is set with
9 pts of line spacing (“nine
over nine”). The text is called
“set solid” when the line

This tightly spaced text is

9/10.8 (auto)
This text uses the default
setting from the page layout
software (120% of the font
size). Although auto-leading
generally works, it produces
awkward sizes like 10.8.
Whole numbers are desirable
in a typographic system.
Spaces for captions, text,
and headlines often have
some numbers in common
(such as 8, 12, 16, and 24).



9/12
Here, the line spacing is
slightly larger than the
default 120%, creating
a more open appearance.
The number 12 is useful
when building a larger
typographic system. This
setting—neither too
cramped nor too loose—
is pleasant to read.

**THE PERILS OF AUTO SPACING**


Auto spacing causes problems when
you start combining multiple sizes
of text. Heads, subheads, and body
copy need to hold together on their
own. The structure of the text falls
apart visually when the space within
a heading is bigger than the space
after it.

**VERTICAL JUSTIFICATION**


Vertical justification fills a
space evenly with lines of
text. Applied to a text frame,
vertical justification can be
useful in specific situations,
such as posters, invitations,
or decorative insets. Vertical
justification can look spotty
or indecisive when applied
throughout a document to
different columns.

thinking with type, third edition 139


VERTICAL SPACE



Paragraph spacing is the vertical space after or before a paragraph. Adding blank zones around paragraphs is the default on
the web. This wastes space and looks gappy. In print, adding a
line after a paragraph is a common alternative to an indent. Adding half a line space instead of a full line saves space and builds a
more compact column.
A baseline shift moves characters up or down in small increments. Use this tool to correct the appearance of uneven spacing
that occurs between varied sizes or styles of type.

position of other lines in the column.

thinking with type, third edition 143


Vertical Text



Chinese and Japanese characters can be read vertically or horizontally; their square forms support beautiful columns. Stacked
Latin letters can serve an abstract visual purpose in headlines and
logotypes. Vertical text often appears on building signs, making
optimal use of narrow urban spaces.
Designing vertical Latin text requires care. Uppercase stacks
are more stable than lowercase. Centering the column helps
compensate for differences in width. A typeface designed with
slab serifs on the letter _I_ widens this skinny character. Another
way to make vertical text is to rotate the line 90 degrees, preserving the natural affinity among letters sitting on a line.

Bilingual | This responsive website
combines Magmatic, a variable Latin
typeface by Cyrus Highsmith with
June Shin (2020), with Gothic MB101,
a Japanese typeface from Morisawa
(1974–2008). The design emphasizes
the difference between the two
writing systems rather than trying to
harmonize them. Website designed
and developed by Marie Otsuka,
Occupant Fonts, 2022.


Optimized for stacking | The typeface Bungee is
inspired by urban bar signs, which often occupy narrow
vertical spaces. Special ligatures such as _IJ_ allow the
skinny _I_ to share space with other letters. In the word
_HAWAIIAN_, two _I_ ’s appear on one line. The full type
family includes features such as vertical kerning and
background shapes. Typeface and specimen designed
by David Jonathan Ross, 2016.

thinking with type, third edition 147


Legibility and
Readability


+ Beatrice Warde, “The Crystal
Goblet, or Why Printing Should
Be Invisible,” in _Graphic Design_
_Theory: Readings from the Field,_
edited by Helen Armstrong (New
York: Princeton Architectural
Press, 2009), 39–43.



To read a text, people must be able to recognize characters and
decode their meaning. Legibility refers to the process of seeing
and identifying words and glyphs. Factors include x-height,
counter size, spacing, character width, and differences among
characters. Readability supports our understanding of a text.
Factors include line spacing, column width, document hierarchy,
and letterform design.
In 1930, type critic Beatrice Warde presented legibility and
readability as opposing concepts. According to Warde, bold,
sans-serif fonts are highly legible, but Baskerville offers a more
pleasing experience. She favored traditional typography, while
the avant-garde designers of her era promoted sans-serif type. [+]

Like most binaries, the opposition between legibility and
readability dissolves under pressure. Many factors contribute
to both optical clarity (legibility) and ease of comprehension
(readability), including size, spacing, and contrast. Familiarity,
physical and mental abilities, and the context and purpose of a
text all affect reading performance and individual preferences.



Distance reading | Legible signs support
public safety and well-being. Signs are
designed to be read quickly from far away.
FF Info employs narrow forms to save space.
Although wide letters are considered more
legible than narrow ones, wider characters
must be displayed smaller to fit in a given
area—making them harder to see.


148 COLUMNS, LINES, AND SPACING



While some designers argue that serifs
make letters more legible by strengthening
their end points, FF Info uses rounded
strokes to embrace the loss of detail in
distance reading. Postcard designed by Erik
Spiekermann. Typeface: FF Info, designed by
Erik Spiekermann and Ole Schäfer, 1996.


**CHARACTER DIFFERENCES**


In the typeface Helvetica Neue (top),
some characters have similar shapes,
such as B/8 or p/q. This can confuse
people with impaired vision or reading
disorders. Atkinson Hyperlegible
(bottom) addresses this problem by
amplifying the differences among
characters.

Atkinson Hyperlegible | _Braille Institute, Applied Design Works, Elliott_
_Scott, Megan Eiswerth, Linus Boman, Theodore Petrosky, 2019_

Love it or leave it | Maximum legibility doesn’t guarantee
an enjoyable experience. The optically distinct characters
of Atkinson Hyperlegible produce a spotty texture, while
the uniform shapes of Helvetica yield smooth, placid fields
of text. The consistency that makes Helvetica comfortably
disappear for some readers is a potential obstacle for others.


Text adapted from Beatrice Warde, “The Crystal Goblet, or Why
Printing Should Be Invisible,” 40–41.



Know what you love | Many people prefer serif fonts for
reading long texts. Some designers believe that serifs improve
legibility because they guide the horizontal movement of the
eye. Research studies have failed to prove, however, that
serifs enhance reading speed or comprehension. Bias favors
familiarity; many people prefer what they see most. [++]


++ Sophie Beier, _Reading Letters: Designing for Legibility_ (Amsterdam:
BIS Publishers, 2012), Sophie Beier, _Type Tricks: User Design_
(Amsterdam: BIS Publishers, 2022), and Charles Bigelow, “Typeface
Features and Legibility Research,” _Vision Research_ 165 (2019): 162–72.



thinking with type, third edition 149


Readable
Prose



×


♥


♥


♥


♥ ♥



Readable text needs more than even kerning and big counter
forms. Typographic beauty can’t fix dull writing. Welcoming
prose uses strong verbs and concrete language. Hostile prose
intimidates readers with pointless details and unfamiliar words.
Sentences are like scenes in a movie. Something happens (an
action), performed by someone or something (an actor). Energize your sentences by asking where the main focus should fall.
Spotlight the actor and the action, and introduce them early in
the scene. Learning to write active, concrete prose will make your
writing easier to understand, more fun to read, and accessible to
more people.


**CHOOSING ACTIVE VERBS**

The dog was on the porch.
The dog waited on the porch.
The dog guarded the porch.
The dog slept on the porch.
The dog grumbled and snored on the porch.


The verb “is/was” conveys an inert state of existence. It lacks action. What is
the dog doing (waiting, guarding, sleeping)? As the verbs become more specific
(grumbling, snoring), the mental images get sharper. Readers don’t just know
that the dog is sleeping—they can see and hear him. This technique, called
“show, don’t tell,” evokes vivid sensations rather than simply stating a fact.


**ABSTRACT, FORMAL WORDS** **CONCRETE, FAMILIAR WORDS**

public spaces —> airports, parks, sidewalks
institutions —> churches, museums, hospitals
pathology —> illness, disease, sickness
transformation —> change, growth, collapse



Abstract words encompass many
ideas or phenomena. This quality
makes them powerful tools. It also
makes these words hard to visualize.



Support abstractions with concrete examples.
Often, you can replace an abstract word with a
more specific or familiar one. Concrete words help
readers see the object or action.



150 COLUMNS, LINES, AND SPACING


×


♥


×


♥



**WRITING ACTIVE, CONCRETE SENTENCES**

Products are designed with materials that are
intended to trigger emotional responses.


Passive and abstract | This dull sentence employs passive voice
(“Products are designed”). Who designed the products? The actor
is missing from the scene. The word _materials_ is abstract. What
materials? No images come to mind. The passive phrase “are intended
to trigger” makes the sentence tentative and wordy.

Wood, glass, stone, and other materials trigger
emotional responses to products.


Active and concrete | The revised sentence uses examples to explain
the abstract word _materials_ . These concrete nouns are the heroes of the
sentence. The verb is active (“trigger,” not “are intended to trigger”).


**SAY MORE WITH LESS**

It has proved difcult to validate through
research that serifs either increase or decrease
reading speed.

Research has failed to prove that serifs either
increase or decrease reading speed.


concise | Replace wordy, passive phrases with short, active ones.


**WRITING HEADLINES**

**DOG SLEEPS ON THE PORCH WHILE**
**ARMED KIDNAPPERS INVADE HOME**
**6-POUND POODLE ATTACKS DELIVERY WORKER**
**SMART DOORBELL REPLACES FAMILY DOG**


Make every word count | Headlines tell a story in a few compact
phrases. A headline doesn’t just describe a scene or state a fact—
it stirs curiosity and implies a bigger action or purpose.

beginner



Experiment with texture by changing the size, spacing, weight,
and style of running text. As letterforms overlap, new relationships emerge between horizontal and vertical strokes. Explore
typographic form by focusing on patterns rather than legibility.

Exercise developed by Karen Kornblum, Associate
Teaching Professor, Carnegie Mellon University



TYPOGRAPHY,
IN THE
NEWTONIAN
VIEW, IS
NOTHING
VERY
INTER


Typography, in the Newtonian view, is nothing very interesting or mysterious; it is simply mechanized writing. Now that the silicon chip has joined the wheel, the lever, and the inclined plane, typography is also computerized, digitized writing: more complex than it was, but no more profound, and perhaps increasingly subject to fashion. Seen with fresher eyes, or from a warier perspective, typography still evokes the wonder and fear with which it startled the medieval world. It is a black art that borders on artificial insemination, and it can pose equally difficult moral questions. Type is writing that is edited, shaped, doctored, and made to reproduce itself through artificial means; and writing itself is a kind of gene-bank for ideas. Confined within the schools, typography is a means of implanting the fruits of chosen minds with the minds and lives of others. Set loose in the world, it is an uncon-trollable vector, like the malaria-bearing mosquito, able to spread ideas as indiscriminately as viruses or germs. The possibilities for its use and abuse are potent and legion. Like the other arts, from medicine to music, typography also demands both close proximity and distance. This is not what it sounds like, a schizophrenic sense of scale, but a kind of taut completeness. Typography is a process, after all, in which large objects - epics, encyclope-dias and bibles, for example - are built from minute components, such as the strokes and bowls of letters. It is work, therefore, in which macroscopic and microscopic perspectives constantly converge. As if that were not enough, it’s also an enterprise in which history is continuously present, and must therefore be kept continuously alive. These are among the things that make it unmechanical and nourishing. Typography, in the Newtonian view, is nothing very interesting or mysterious; it is simply mechanized writing. Now that the silicon chip has joined the wheel, the lever, and the inclined plane, typography is also computerized, digitized writing: more complex than it was, but no more profound, and perhaps increasingly subject to fashion. _make it unmechanical and nourishing. Typography, in the Newtonian view, is nothing very interesting or mysterious;_



_it is simply mechanized writing. Now that the silicon chip has joined the wheel, the lever, and the inclined plane,_



_typography is also computerized, digitized writing: more complex than it was, but no more profound, and perhaps in-_

_creasingly subject to fashion. Seen with fresher eyes, or from a warier perspective, typography still evokes the wonder_



_and fear with which it startled the medieval world. It is a black art that borders on artificial insemination, and it can_

Minimal hierarchy (above) | This book
design employs two sizes and one weight
of type. Distributing content spatially on a
grid reveals the book’s parts, setting apart
the title, subhead, running head, body
text, and footnotes. _Data Centers_ (Lars
Müller Publishers, 2021), edited by Monika
Dommann, Hannes Rickli, and Max Stadler.
Designed by Hubertus Design.


Layered hierarchy (right) | This ebook
features a layered hierarchy with several
styles, weights, and sizes of type. Rejecting
traditional patterns of dominance, the
numbered section markers are taller and
bolder than the main headline. The short
subtitles summarize the long text below,
allowing readers to choose how far to
read. _Black Archives Editorial 001_ (Black
Archives, 2020), edited by Renata Cherlise
and Savannah Wood. Designed by Renald
Louissant.

this entrance ticket for an

women in Switzerland
(SAFFA, 1958); she used a

to work with Jacqueline

There, Moll introduced her

and flush-left typography. [+]

flat hierarchy | This
portfolio website arranges

three-column grid. The big,
uniform type underscores

Design: Typical Organization
for Standards & Order.



One way to create a typographic hierarchy is to begin with one
size and style of typeface and build variations. In the 1940s,
Swiss graphic designers constructed minimal hierarchies with
just a few sizes and weights of type. Those experiments, which
emphasize spatial arrangement, still fascinate designers today.
Beginning with a plain block of text, introduce variables to
separate the parts of the text. Changing the scale, weight, position, or color of text helps reveal the structure without adding
more typefaces.

Text adapted from Max Bill,

on typography
it’s worth taking a look once again at the state of typography
­to­day. when one does this as an outsider, who occupies himself
more with the stylistic characteristics of the epoch than with the
ephemeral manifestations of momentary fashion, and if one sees
in typography primarily a means of creating cultural documents
and of allowing con­temporary products to become cultural
documents, than one can impar­tially deal with the problems which
grow out of typographic material.— max bill

In a bowl of salad or pasta, diverse ingredients offer harmony and
contrast, while textures on top make the dish sparkle and pop.
A layered hierarchy celebrates contrast and variety. Select a few
ingredients, handle them gently, and serve them with love.
Ice cream sundaes follow a basic template: start with a bland
base, add a rich sauce, and finish up with a crunchy topping. [+]
The ice cream sundae template makes beautiful typography, too!
Each part contributes to the whole: digestible text, intense headlines, and a light shower of crisp details. Don’t forget the crunchy
bits—many new designers leave this ingredient out. Visual texture pulls a design together.



**ICE CREAM SUNDAE TEMPLATE**

A type scale is a selection of type sizes based on history or mathematics. The preset sizes built into many page layout applications
reflect historical type sizes, established for letterpress printing
in the sixteenth century. [+] Online type scale calculators generate ​
sizes using ratios from music or mathematics, such as the Fibonacci sequence or the golden ratio. [++] The calculator starts with
the body size as the base, such as 10-pt text for print or 16-px
text for screen. Then the calculator multiplies the base and each
successive type size by that ratio. For example, using the ratio 2:3
and a 10-pt base, the calculator will multiply each size by 150 percent, beginning with size 10. Type scale calculators can employ
any unit, including inches, points, pixels, ems, and rems.
Working with type scales is a matter of personal preference.
Some designers love connecting typography with music, nature,
mathematics, and print history. Type scales can feel limiting,
however, especially when combining a variety of typefaces with
distinct proportions.

Major third | The type scales shown
here were made with Modular Scale
(modularscale.com), a calculator created
by Scott Kellum and Tim Brown. The major
third (4:5) is a ratio borrowed from music.
This ratio generates a gradual series of
sizes, useful for a typographic system.

Golden ratio | The number 1.618 belongs
to the golden ratio, a proportional number
series found throughout art and nature. The
big jumps produced by this ratio are too
dramatic for most type systems. Some type
scale calculators introduce a third variable
to slow down the progression of each ratio.

thinking with type, third edition 165


Visual and
Semantic
Hierarchy


Tagging the parts of the
document make it accessible
to a screen reader, which
vocalizes the text in a linear
order. The text at right has no

breaks.





Visual hierarchy employs size, weight, style, color, and position
to express a document’s structure. Semantic hierarchy assigns
tags to content elements to signal their function and importance. Semantic elements in HTML include page regions such
as <main>, <body>, <header>, <footer>, <nav>, and <aside>.
Headings include <title>, <h1> through <h6>, and lists. Print
designers can link paragraph styles to HTML tags to create accessible digital documents.
Most accessible documents use both visual and semantic hierarchies. People who read visually need visual cues; auditory
readers (and search engines) need semantic tags. Clear structures help humans and machines find and understand content.




```
<body>

```

thinking with type, third edition 167


Inclusive
Design



Creating content that everyone can understand is called inclusive design. A person’s educational background, language skills,
physical abilities, and cognitive and sensory abilities affect how
they read text. Many people access text through screen readers
(which vocalize text) and other assistive devices. Designers and
writers should describe images for people who cannot see them.
Numerous books and guides discuss accessible design in
depth. Below are some tips for designing content that welcomes
people from many different backgrounds and abilities. [+]



+ “Accessibility Funda­mentals,” **TIPS FOR DESIGNING INCLUSIVE CONTENT**
W3C Web Accessibility Initiative,
www.w3.org/WAI/fundamentals; Heydon Pickering, _Inclusive Design_ Write in **plain language**
_Patterns_ (Freiburg, Germany: familiar words to reach everyone.
Smashing Magazine, 2016);
University of Cambridge, _Inclusive_ Explain abstract ideas with **examples**
_Design Toolkit_, 2015, www
.inclusivedesigntoolkit.com;
_Federal Plain Language Guidelines_, Use **images**
March 2011, www.plainlanguage.
gov/guidelines; Bradley Nice, Write **alternate text** and
“Creating Accessible Documents,”
June 4, 2016, bradley-nice. Provide **captions** for audio content.
medium.com/creating-accessible[documents-213f2f678bdd#.](http://medium.com/creating-accessibledocuments-213f2f678bdd#.l3zr7ail8)
l3zr7ail8. Apply **headings**



Write in **plain language** . Use simple sentences and
familiar words to reach everyone.



Explain abstract ideas with **examples** .



Use **images** to support (but not replace) text.



Write **alternate text** and **visual descriptions** for images.



Provide **captions** for audio content.



Apply **headings** (H1, H2, etc.) to the parts of a document.



atkinson hyperlegible
_Braille Institute, Applied_
_Design Works, Elliott Scott,_
_Megan Eiswerth, Linus_
_Boman, Theodore Petrosky,_
_2019_



Break long content into **lists** where appropriate.


Create **captions** and **sidebars** .


Use **labels** to identify columns and rows in tables.


Write **specific text** for buttons and links (not “click here”).


Use **strong dark/light contrast** to help readers see text.
Avoid using color to convey essential information.


Declare a document’s **language** so that screen reader software
will apply the correct voice profile.


Ensure that the elements of a PDF are in the **correct order** for
navigating with a screen reader or keyboard.

thinking with type, third edition 169


**exercise**
Grids and
Hierarchy


intermediate



This exercise has three phases. Start with a modular grid and one
size and style of type; use space to reveal the parts of the text.
Next, sharpen the hierarchy with contrasting scales, weights, and
styles of type. Finally, use color, shapes, lines, and other elements
to bring sensory and narrative qualities to the design. Grids support iterative design (creating multiple layouts or prototypes).
A recipe is a short text with many parts: title, blurb, ingredients, and instructions. A single recipe can become a template for
a publication or website. Food is personal. Choose a recipe that
reflects your food pleasures, cultural heritage, family traditions,
food politics, or culinary creativity.




|Col1|Col2|Col3|Col4|Col5|

|skylar nguyen | R<br>Trang, runawayrice|ecipe by<br> .com||||
|170<br>|||||
|170<br>||HIERAR|CHY AND S|CHY AND S|


**1. BASIC GRID**


First, create a grid with twelve columns
and an appropriate number of rows.
(This square grid has twelve rows.)
Set your text in a single size and style
of type. Break the content into parts,
and use the grid to organize and align
them. Produce several variations and
choose one to develop further.


**2. ADD SCALE**


Add emphasis with one or more
contrasting scales, weights, and styles
of type. Make multiple compositions
using these elements.


**3. ADD COLOR AND FORM**


Use color to enhance the emotional
impact of the composition and
emphasize or downplay content.
Optionally, create expressive elements
that illustrate your recipe and interact
with the type and the grid.





Squash, Red Lentil & Coconut Curry a Hemsley + Hemsley RecipeDF VEGAN V GF


This is a simple, no fuss, one pot, colourful supper and children love the sweetness from the coconut and butternut squash. We’ve been making versions of this dish for the last 4 years – friends love it because it’s tasty, healthy and easy to knock together – in fact this little curry was the inspiration for our cooking classes and later – our com-pany.



chicken bone brothIngredientsServes 122 medium organic butternut squash7 cloves organic garlic, chopped6 inches fresh ginger, grated or finely chopped6 medium organic onions, chopped2.2 - 2½ litres homemade chicken bone broth*500g organic red split lentils1 tsp black pepper1 bar organic creamed coconut2 tsp or more turmericOrganic baby spinach (you can also use cabbage/seasonal greens) sliced2 tsp sea salt2 small limes or 1½ large lemons, juiced150g (or 4 large handfuls) fresh coriander, washed wellOptional ½ tsp cayenne or chilli flakesTip*For a vegan or vegetarian option, use agood-quality vegetable stock instead of the



How To:1. Peel the squash and dice into 1 inch chunks2. Place the garlic, onion, ginger and squash into the panwith the creamed coconut and cover with just over 2 litres(8 cups) of water or homemade bone broth.3. Put the lid on and bring to a medium simmer.4. 10 minutes later, add the red lentils, black pepper, tur-meric and chilli if using, stir and let simmer on medium fora further 15-20 minutes until the lentils are soft and thesquash is tender. You might wish to add the extra 200ml (1cup) of water during cooking – it depends if you like yourstew thicker or thinner.5. In the last few minutes, add the sliced cabbage/greensand stir through. If using spinach, just add when you turnoff the heat so it wilts6. Turn off the heat, add the sea salt and pepper, the juiceof the lime or lemon and check for seasoning and consis-tency – it should be like a thick stew – add more water ifneeds be.7. Stir through the roughly chopped coriander and ladle intoshallow bowls to serve.



**SQUASH,**
**RED LENTIL &**
**COCONUT CURRY**


**DF VEGAN V GF** **INGREDIENTS** **HOW TO**



medium organic butternut squashcloves organic garlic, choppedinches fresh ginger, grated or finely choppedmedium organic onions, choppedhomemade chicken bone broth*organic red split lentilsblack pepperorganic creamed coconuttsp or more turmericOrganic baby spinach slicedsea saltsmall limes, juicedfresh coriander, washed well ½ tsp cayenne or chilli flakes*For a vegan or vegetarian option, use agood-quality vegetable stock instead of thechicken bone broth



a Hemsley + Hemsley Recipe

Serves 12This is a simple, no fuss, one pot, colourful supper and children love the sweetness from the coconut and butternut squash. We’ve been making versions of this dish for the last 4 years friends love it because it’s tasty, healthy and easy to knock together – in fact this little curry was the inspiration for our cooking classes and later – our company.



6 inches7 cloves 2

2½ litres 500g1 bar2 tsp1 tsp

Optional ½ tsp 2 tsp150gTip2



1 Peel the squash and dice into 1 inch chunks

2 Place the garlic, onion, ginger and squash into the pan with the creamed coconut and cover with just over 2 litres (8 cups) of water or homemade bone broth.


3 Put the lid on and bring to a medium simmer.

4 10 minutes later, add the red lentils, black pepper, turmeric and chilli if using, stir and let simmer on medium for a further 15-20 minutes until the lentils are soft and the squash is tender. You might wish to add the extra 200ml (1 cup) of water during cooking – it depends if you like your stew thicker or thinner.

5 In the last few minutes, add the sliced cabbage/greens and stir through. If using spinach, just add when you turn off the heat so it wilts

6 Turn off the heat, add the sea salt and pepper, the juice of the lime or lemon and check for seasoning and consistency—it should be like a thick stew—add more water if needs be.

7 Stir through the roughly chopped coriander and ladle into shallow bowls to serve.

172


Abdoulaye and Ibrahima Barry created the
ADLaM script in the 1980s. They continue to refine
the script, which flourishes in Fulani communities
across West Africa. The Barry brothers worked with
type designers Mark Jamra and Neil Patel to design



an ADLaM complement for Kigelia, a large type
family containing Africa’s most prominent writing
systems. The Barrys wrote notes and instructions
to Jamra and Patel on this proof (above) to indicate
changes and refinements in the letterforms.



thinking with type, third edition 173


Arabic
Typography

WRITTEN AND
DESIGNED BY
KHAJAG APELIAN
AND WAEL MORCOS



The Arabic script evolved from imperfect handwriting to an elevated art with elaborate rules for letter shaping, composition,
rhythm, and harmony. Today, this phonetic script is the official
language of twenty-two countries and the third most widely used
writing system in the world, following Latin and Chinese.
Arabic and Latin typography share many design principles,
such as grids, columns, type size, and line spacing. While Latin
uses italics for in-line emphasis, Arabic can deploy a different
calligraphic style or a color change.
The Rosetta Stone is a famous trilingual inscription, carved
in Memphis, Egypt, in 196 BCE. Its text is written in Ancient
Egyptian hieroglyphic and demotic scripts and Ancient Greek.
Today, multiscript typesetting is more common than ever before. Understanding some of the mechanics of Arabic scripts can
help designers create layouts that honor historical conventions.



Multilingual layout | In this specimen
booklet for Graphik Arabic, a typeface
published by Commercial Type, the text is set
in Arabic (flush right) and English (flush left).


174 MULTIPLICITY OF SCRIPTS



Graphik Arabic | _Khajag Apelian,_
_Wael Morcos, 2017_
Graphik Latin | _Christian_
_Schwartz, 2009_


**RIGHT TO LEFT**


Arabic words and sentences
read from right to left. Thus,
the natural alignment for Arabic
is flush right. Arabic books are
bound with the spine to the right.


**CONNECTED SCRIPT**


There is no upper- or lowercase in
Arabic, but there are multiple ways
to write a single letter depending
on its position in the word. Here,
a single character (shown in red)
changes based on its position:
initial, medial, final, or isolated.


**BASE SHAPE AND**
**DIACRITICS**


Many letters in Arabic share the
same base drawing (rasm) and are
distinguished only by the number
and placement of dots above or
below it. At this microlevel, Arabic
is modular—many components are
repeated to form more complex
characters. A page of Arabic text
can look elaborate and organic, but
the system is actually predictable.


**SHORT AND LONG VOWELS**


The Arabic script consists of twentynine letters. Twenty-six of them
are consonants and only three are
vowels:  [ _alif_ ],  [ _waw_ ], and [ _ya’a_ ].
Long vowels are placed between
consonants, whereas short vowels,
which are smaller, are placed above
or under a consonant. In everyday
use, Arabic often is written without
the short vowel signs because the
context of the sentence helps identify
the word. Phonetic writing systems
consisting of consonants (or mostly
consonants) are called abjads.



LYON ARABIC | _Khajag Apelian, Wael Morcos, 2020_

_(all Arabic on this page)_



thinking with type, third edition 175


ARABIC TYPOGRAPHY


**TATWEEL OR MADD**


It is common to elongate the connection between
two Arabic characters. This practice, known
as tatweel or madd, is used to letterspace and
justify lines. Designers also use _tatweel_ for
emphasis in book or chapter titles. Tatweel is
sometimes called kashida, a Persian word that
means “extended.”


**NUMERALS**


The Arabic numerals used in Latin typography
(0123456789) are also known as Hindu-Arabic
numerals. This number system originated in
India and was introduced to Europe in the
tenth century CE by the Arabic speakers of
North Africa, who used these digits in a region
spanning present-day Libya to Morocco. The
numerals used in the Middle East today are
Eastern Arabic numerals. These numbers read
from left to right.


arabic (hindu-Arabic)


Hindi (eastern Arabic)


Farsi (perso-arabic)



LYON ARABIC | _Khajag Apelian, Wael Morcos, 2020_


**LETTER FUSIONS AND WORD SHAPING**


Arabic is organic. Letters come together to form
new fused combinations, which are necessary for
correct grammar. Fused letters (ligatures) have
direct consequences on word shaping, which in
turn affects the readability of the text. Furthermore,
fused letters influence the white space around words
and the overall texture of a paragraph. Thanks to
digital technology, Arabic fonts can contain complex
combinations, emulating the rich texture of the
written script.



Brando serif | _Mike Abbink, 2014_ LYON ARABIC | _Khajag Apelian, Wael Morcos, 2020_
Brando ARABIC | _Khajag Apelian, Wael Morcos, 2020_


176 MULTIPLICITY OF SCRIPTS


**LANGUAGES AND SCRIPTS**


The Arabic script is used for writing
Arabic and several other languages of
Asia and Africa, including Persian, Urdu,
Pashto, and Punjabi, to name a few.


**CLASSIFICATION**


Arabic type falls into two categories:
Layyen (soft or fluid construction) and
Yabes (solid construction). Al-Khatt
AL-Yabes contains mainly geometric,
Kufic-based scripts. Al-Khatt ALLayyen contains many scripts that
have a more organic flow, such as
thuluth, diwani, or nastaliq. Naskhi,
the most common of the fluid scripts,
is generally used for text. Designers
combine the styles for various
reasons, such as creating information
hierarchies or hinting at cultural and
aesthetic origins.


mizan | _Kristyan Sarkis, 2018_


Sf mada | _Sultan M. Saeed, 2017_


favorit arabic | _Khajag Apelian, Wael_

_Morcos, 2021_


Fresco Arabic | _Lara Assouad Khoury,_

_Fred Smeijers, 2007_


KUFAM Arabic | _Wael Morcos, 2016_


BUKRA | _Pascal Zoghbi, 2017_



IBM Plex Arabic | _Khajag Apelian, Wael Morcos, 2020_


Fluid style based on Nastaliq script

Noto Nastaliq Urdu | _Google Fonts_


Fluid style based on Ruqaa script

felfel | _Abdo Mohamed, 2021_


Fluid styles based on Naskhi script


Solid styles based on Kufic script



thinking with type, third edition 177


Chinese
Typography


WRITTEN BY
CASPAR LAM AND
YUJUNE PARK


DIAGRAMS BY
NAIQIAN WANG AND
STEPHANIE WINARTO


**PHONO-SEMANTIC**



The first examples of Chinese writing were inscribed on animal
bones used for divination in the late Shang dynasty, which rose to
power during the second half of the second millennium BCE. The
characters on these oracle bones evolved into modern Chinese,
whose forms are influenced by the writing brush.
Calligraphy and the written word hold enormous significance
in Chinese culture. This material and cultural ecosystem influenced the invention and development of printing in China, which
favored woodblock printing. Western-style presses began to be
adopted in the nineteenth century. Separately, language reforms
occurred in the twentieth century as the country grappled with
modernity. Thus, the process of typesetting written Chinese is
relatively new.
In Chinese, characters are called Hanzi, where _Han_ means
“Han Chinese” and _zi_ means “word” or “character.” Historically,
Chinese characters have been employed in the writing systems
of neighboring countries, including Korea, Japan, and Vietnam,
where they are called Hanja, kanji, and chữ Hán, respectively.



The basic unit of written Chinese is a
character, which is represented as a glyph
in a typeface. Single or multiple characters
can form a word. Some characters can be
classified as pictograms and ideograms
(depicting an object or idea). The vast
majority of characters, however, are phonosemantic (also called logosyllabic).


178 MULTIPLICITY OF SCRIPTS



Phono-semantic characters contain a
component that signifies an approximate
sound and a component that signifies an
approximate meaning. While there are
over 80,000 extant characters, fewer than
10,000 are in use. A display typeface may
contain only a few thousand characters.

**TRADITIONAL, SIMPLIFIED, AND**
**VARIANT CHARACTERS**


Modern Chinese has two orthographies or
written versions of the script: traditional
and simplified. Simplified Chinese grew
out of early twentieth-century language
reform movements. Promulgated in 1956,
simplified Chinese is used in mainland
China, Singapore, and the United Nations,
while traditional Chinese is used in Hong
Kong, Taiwan, and many overseas Chinese
communities. Characters in simplified
Chinese are generally written with fewer
strokes than their traditional counterparts.
Both traditional and simplified Chinese
have variant versions of themselves. These
nonstandard ways of writing characters have
arisen over the course of history.

Strokes of the same type can vary their
shape when they interact with other strokes
to form a character. Complex characters
are often made from many component
groupings of strokes and even from other
characters. A word consists of one or more
characters.

CHINESE TYPOGRAPHY


**TEXTURE**


Chinese typefaces are monospaced
(each character and punctuation mark
occupies the same sized block). There
are no word spaces in Chinese. The
density of characters varies from one
stroke to more than thirty strokes, giving
Chinese a darker typographic color than
many other written languages.


**READING AND TYPESETTING**


The eye moves from center of gravity
to center of gravity during the
reading process. Because Chinese
characters do not sit on a baseline,
their internal axes are used for
alignment. Historically, the script is
read vertically (top to bottom, right to
left). Horizontal reading (left to right,
top to bottom) is common today.


center of
gravity



vertical density



Twenty to thirty characters
are commonly seen in a line of
body text, which is usually fully
justified. However, shorter texts
can be left aligned. Line spacing
for Chinese texts tends to be
greater than their alphabetic
counterparts, while the length of
the overall text tends to be about
half that of English texts.

gravity

180 MULTIPLICITY OF SCRIPTS


**CALLIGRAPHY, PRINTED TYPE,**
**AND MEISHUZI**


The visual form of the Chinese script pivots
around three overlapping aesthetic concepts
that are tied to their historical methods of
production: calligraphy, printed type, and
meishuzi. Calligraphic forms have developed
from the interactions between brush, ink,
and substrate, while printed forms have
developed in response to the printing
process. Printed typefaces have mimicked
calligraphic forms, but not the other
way around. Meishuzi (artistic styles) are
characterized by visual experimentation and
are used mostly for display purposes. The use
of these terms tends to be blurry. Today, all
three forms appear in digital typefaces.


**CLASSIFICATION**


The four commonly acknowledged families
of printed typefaces are Regular Script,
Songti, Imitation Song, and Heiti. Regular
Script (Kaiti) is based on a calligraphic
style that matured in the Tang dynasty and
was later used in printed materials. It is
considered the most traditional typeface
style and serves as a reference for the
canonical shape of Chinese characters.
Songti (also called Mingti) was the first
true Chinese typeface to appear in print. Its
contrasting, rectilinear forms and triangular
terminals are associated with books.
Imitation Song, which first appeared as a
revival in the twentieth century, combines
the textual qualities of Songti with the
handwritten slant of Regular Script. Its uses
are varied. For example, in mainland China,
government documents are often set in
Imitation Song.
Heiti styles, which appeared in the 1930s,
were first imported from Japan. They are
derived from European grotesque (sansserif) typefaces. They were initially used for
display, but they have since seen broad use
across different contexts.

King Sejong the Great invented the Hangeul writing system
(also spelled Hangul and called Chosongul in North Korea) in
1443. Originally named Hunminjeongeum, meaning “the proper sounds for the instruction of the people,” it became known as
Hangeul, meaning “Great Script.” The book _Hunminjeongeum_,
published by King Sejong in 1446, describes the purpose, phonology, linguistics, and design of the new writing system, which
enabled the Korean people to write their language phonetically
and achieve better communication, education, and prosperity.
In South Korea, Hangeul has been modernized in the years
since 1945, coinciding with social, cultural, and democratic
movements. Hangeul characters and type designs have gained
widespread visibility through the Korean Wave, visual pop culture, digital fonts, and various media that pair Hangeul and Latin fonts for global audiences.



**THE FIVE BASIC CONSONANTS**


Hangeul is a phonogramic language, which represents vocal
sounds, and the sounds denote meanings. The Hangeul
letterforms for the five basic consonants and elements
(ㄱ, ㄴ, ㅁ, ㅅ, and ㅇ) were designed based on their sounds
and on the shapes created by the vocal organs.

Hunminjeongeum (1446) _._ This page from
the book announcing the invention of
Hangeul describes some plain consonants
and uses. _Hunminjeongeum_ demonstrates
that Hangeul was proclaimed in print
before Hangeul’s handwriting came into
use. The surrounding text employs Chinese
characters, which were borrowed for
writing the Korean language before the
creation of Hangeul. Woodblock print from
the Digital Hangeul Museum, South Korea.


Worincheongangjigok (vol. 1, 1447) is one
of the first texts published using Hangeul
movable metal type. The title means “Songs
of the Moon’s Reflection on a Thousand
Rivers.” This Buddhist hymnbook was written
and published in memory of Queen Soheon.
The book is listed as a National Treasure
in recognition of its importance to Korean
linguistics, the history of printing, and the
early development of Hangeul.

KOREAN TYPOGRAPHY


**CONSONANT LETTERFORMS**


Today, Hangeul employs twenty-four basic
letters, including fourteen consonants and
eleven vowels. (The original system utilized
twenty-eight letters.)
The chart at right shows how letterforms
were built in relation to sounds and
shapes. Nine additional consonants were
designed by adding strokes to the five
basic consonants in the first column. For
example, the bilabial consonants ㅂ and ㅍ
were constructed by adding strokes to ㅁ,
shown in the third row. Five double (twin)
consonants (marked with an asterisk) were
designed by repeating a basic consonant.
For example, the design of ㅃ repeats the
letter ㅂ. Letters no longer used in modern
Hangeul are shown in gray.

**VOWEL LETTERFORMS**


The three basic vowel elements refer to cosmic
philosophy. . depicts the shape of heaven,

- [represents the flatness of Earth, and ][। ][is a ]
human standing on Earth. Conceptually, the
ten basic vowels were designed by combining
one or two dots (heaven) and a line (Earth and
human). In addition to the ten basic vowels,
the eleven double vowels were formed by
combining basic vowels, such as adding । to
to make  or adding — to । to create ˧. The
vowels shown in the chart were original designs
printed in _Hunminjeongeum_ . The twenty-one
vowels shown in black are still used today. In
modern Hangeul writing and font designs, the
dot becomes a short stem.


Diagrams based on _The Standard Commentary of_
_Hunminjeongeum_, National Hangeul Museum, 2017, and
Jaejoon Han, _Hangeul Design Textbook_, 2009.

KOREAN TYPOGRAPHY


**CONFIGURATION SYSTEM**


Hangeul letters are assembled like building
blocks. An initial consonant is called a datja
(닿자), a medial vowel is called a holja (홀자), and
a final consonant is called a batchimdatja
(받침닿자). These letters are combined to create
a larger character, called an onja (온자).
A minonja (민온자) is an onja that ends with a
vowel (holja). A batchimonja (받침온자) is an onja
that ends with a consonant (batchimdatja). The
arrangement of a minonja or a batchimonja can
be horizontal, vertical, or mixed. This system
is determined by the position of the holja and
double holja.
To account for every character in the Unicode
standard, a complete Hangeul font consists of
nineteen initial consonants, twenty-one medial
vowels, and twenty-seven final consonants,
which assemble to create 11,172 characters. Many
Hangeul fonts provide 2,350 characters, covering
those that are most frequently used.

186 MULTIPLICITY OF SCRIPTS


**SQUARE/DE-SQUARED**


Hangeul typefaces can be designed to fit in
square forms or de-squared forms. In the
traditional square form, the letter ㅎ changes
its size, position, and proportions when
combined with other letters to make the
characters 학 and 훼.
In contrast, the typeface AG Ahnsangsoo
introduces a de-squared form. Here, ㅎ
maintains its size and proportion when
assembled with other letters. However, the
character 학 becomes taller than the square
form because the vertical stem of the medial
vowel (holja) extends to meet the center of
the final consonant (ㄱ). Therefore, the final
consonant (batchimdatja) is placed outside
the square frame.

**COMPARING DENSITY**


Hangeul text generally uses less space
than Latin because of the stacked
letters. Unlike Latin, the widths and
heights of Hangeul letters maintain
constant brightness and darkness
because there are no ascenders or
descenders. Hangeul typefaces have
minimal up-and-down movement
within a line of text. Most Hangeul
fonts are designed with monospaced
characters having the same width.

KOREAN TYPOGRAPHY


**READING DIRECTION**


Hangeul words and sentences read
either horizontally and left to right
or vertically and right to left. Since
the 1940s, most Korean publications
and newspapers have adapted the
horizontal direction. However, the
vertical direction still appears in
tall writing spaces or for aesthetic
purposes.


**CLASSIFICATION**


Hangeul fonts can be classified several
ways. The two main styles are Batang

[바탕], also called Myeongjo [명조],
and Dotum [돋움], also called Gothic.
Batang is similar to serif typefaces
in Latin, and Dotum is analogous to
sans serif. Additional styles include
calligraphic or script-based typefaces,
such as Gungseo [궁서], de-squared
typefaces, and display typefaces.
(These categories are not the only way
to organize Hangeul typefaces.)
The Batang and Latin serif styles,
which are commonly used for body text,
share attributes such as the relationship
between thick and thin strokes. However,
the history and development of these
type styles are different.

188 MULTIPLICITY OF SCRIPTS


**HANGEUL AND LATIN TEXT**


When Hangeul and Latin texts are set together in type
with the same type size, line spacing, and tracking,
the texture and length will be different. A paragraph of
Hangeul text will run shorter than a paragraph written
in English, but the characters look bigger and darker
because of Hangeul’s configuration system. To balance
the two texts, designers should consider not only the look



of the letterforms but also the different spaces, textures,
and intensities of color they create. The bilingual pairings
below demonstrate some adjustments. For example, in the
Hangeul settings, the font sizes are slightly smaller, but
the line spaces are larger. The Latin baselines are shifted
up to align the Latin ascenders and descenders in the first
line with the top and bottom lines of Hangeul’s first line.

WRITTEN AND

RYOKO NISHIZUKA


The Sarashina Diary
(Sarashina Nikki).
Handwritten by Fujiwara no

The Japanese script uses two types of characters: ideographic and
syllabic. Ideographic kanji characters were introduced from China in the first century CE; they became widely used around the
sixth to seventh centuries, reflecting the spread of Buddhism and
other religions. Japanese manyogana characters were based on
kanji characters, using the sounds from the Chinese characters
to represent sounds in the Japanese language.
Hiragana characters, a simplified form of manyogana, were
created in the ninth century. In the tenth century and later, many
diaries, essays, and stories were written using hiragana characters. Katakana characters also originated around this time. It is
said that katakana characters were written by omitting stroke
elements from manyogana in order to save space.
Traditionally, Japanese people wrote in vertical lines with a
brush, but with the spread of imported books written in Western languages after the Meiji Restoration (1868–1889), people
began writing in horizontal lines with a pen as well. Tsukiji Type
Foundry, one of the first Japanese type foundries, appeared in
the 1860s, accelerating a move away from woodblock printing.

**DESIGNING KANJI GLYPHS**









modern design


Traditional design







A kanji character uses a combination of stroke
elements commonly shared with other kanji
characters. Depending on the number of strokes
and the size of the counter spaces, the thickness of
each stroke needs to be adjusted. Modern typeface
designs have larger counter spaces and simpler,
more geometric stroke elements. Traditional designs
tend to have smaller counter spaces and more



organically shaped stroke elements. The names
of some character elements are based on the
hierarchy of arranging elements to form a character,
while other names are based on the shapes of the
elements themselves.


Source Han Serif ExtraLight | _Ryoko Nishizuka_
_(art direction, kanji and kana design), 2014_



**DESIGNING HIRAGANA AND KATAKANA GLYPHS**



Because hiragana and katakana characters are more
organic than kanji characters, it is effective to use
shapes formed by movements of the hand. Minchostyle typefaces, which use more complex strokes
and elements, are often drawn first with a brush. The
digital outlines are adjusted later.



Source Han Serif ExtraLight | _Ryoko Nishizuka_
_(art direction, kanji and kana design), 2014_

JAPANESE TYPOGRAPHY


**VERTICAL GLYPHS**


In most cases, the same shape of a
character (glyph) is used for both the
vertical and horizontal writing modes.
However, some characters, such as
punctuation marks and small kana
characters, require glyphs specifically
designed for the vertical writing mode.
If these writing-mode–dependent
characters are used, when the mode
is switched from horizontal to vertical,
the glyphs are automatically replaced.

Latin glyphs included in a Japanese font are
usually aligned 120 units from the bottom,
assuming that the size of the Japanese type
body is 1,000 units. In some cases, designers
will want to change the size or position of
the Latin glyphs, but this must be done with
care. If the Latin glyphs are increased in size
to optically match the kanji characters, the
Latin glyphs may look too large.

**NUMBERS IN VERTICAL LINES**


In vertical lines, numbers are usually written
in kanji numeral glyphs. However, for
numbers with two or fewer digits, HinduArabic numeral glyphs are sometimes used
in the upright posture. Latin characters are
usually rotated ninety degrees in a vertical
line to align the direction of characters to
that of the Japanese vertical line. However, in
the case of short words or abbreviations with
only two or three characters, Latin characters
also may be typeset in the upright posture
in a vertical line. This mode of typesetting is
called tate-chu-yoko (meaning “a horizontal
line in a vertical line”).

**KERNING KANA CHARACTERS**
**FONT-SIDE SETTING VALUES**



Especially in display lines, headlines,
and in larger type sizes, words composed
of hiragana or katakana characters may
look too loose. There are cases where the
font can produce tighter, optically better
spacing. Proportional metrics information
enables the use of an alternative set of glyph
widths and side-bearings. Kerning pair
information adjusts the space that is applied
between two particular glyphs.

_and kana), Robert Slimbach (Latin), 2017_

on the traditional em-square. Japanese
variable fonts have also been developed.

printer’s flowers can form larger shapes and
ornaments. Glyph variations with interesting
effects can be triggered automatically by the
combination of preceding and succeeding
characters.

Indic
Typography


WRITTEN AND
DESIGNED BY
SHIVA NALLAPERUMAL



India is the most linguistically diverse nation in the world. The
2001 census identified 122 major languages and 1,599 other languages in use across the country. Apart from Urdu, which is based
on the Arabic nastaliq script, all Indic scripts are syllabaries that
use vowels, consonants, conjuncts, and matras (diacritic marks).
Each unit expresses a base consonant sound and a secondary
vowel sound; the consonant form is modified to create different
vowel sounds. This type of writing system, called an abugida, is
phonetic and modular, making for large and complex character
sets with a clear and logical structure.
Indic scripts fall into two broad groups: North Indian scripts
(Indo-European) and South Indian scripts (Dravidian). Devanagari, a North Indian script, is the world’s fourth most widely
adopted writing system. It supports more than 120 languages,
including Hindi and Marathi. Bangla, the world’s fifth most
widely used script, supports Bengali, Assamese, and other
languages. Other major North Indian scripts include Gurmukhi
(used to write the widely spoken Punjabi language), Gujarati,
Odia, and Urdu. South Indian scripts include Tamil, Malayalam,
Kannada, and Telugu. Dravidian languages cannot easily be connected to other language families, so scholars believe that they
are indigenous to the Indian subcontinent.
Owing to India’s colonized past, English is one of the official
languages of the nation. English remains a major means of
communication, especially in the urban centers. Bilingual and
trilingual typesetting is common and encouraged (especially in
signage). Most modern Indic typefaces include a matching Latin
character set or are designed to match an existing Latin typeface.
This introduction showcases some of the major Indic scripts.
Numerous minority scripts are threatened by extinction. Many
linguists, publishers, and type designers are working to preserve
India’s endangered scripts and expand their use.

**STRESS AND CONTRAST**


The traditional shapes of Indic scripts are defined by the
tools used by early scribes. In the calligraphic tradition
of Indo-European or North Indian scripts, letterforms are
written with broad-nib pens. Devanagari, Bangla, and
Gujarati are constructed with a 45˚ angled stress. The Latin
angle mirrors the Indic one.



lava Devanagari
_Parimal Parmar, 2019_


lava Telugu
_Ramakrishna Saiteja, 2019_



lava
_Peter Biľak, 2013_


lava



Dravidian or South Indian scripts were traditionally written
with styluses rather than broad-nib pens, creating monoline
forms, which lack contrast. Early colonial type founders
added contrast to scripts such as Telugu (above). The play
of contrast in these formerly monoline forms has been
experimented with and stylistically stretched by generations
of Indian letterers, illustrators, and sign painters, providing
today’s type designers with rich and plural precedents.

INDIC TYPOGRAPHY


**STYLES**


Today, Indic fonts come in three broad
styles: traditional (similar to Latin serif
typefaces); modern (similar to Latin
sans serifs), and display (free-form
interpretations with various sources).

196 MULTIPLICITY OF SCRIPTS


**MATRAS**


Matras are diacritic characters
added to base glyphs to change
their vowel sound. For example,
adding the i-matra to _ka_
changes the sound to _ki_ .

Greta Sans Devanagari
Ka i-Matra Ki
_Hitesh Malaviya, 2017_


**CONJUNCTS**



Indic scripts are syllabaries; the
consonants have an inherent
_a_ sound. Conjuncts are letter
combinations that produce
accurate phonetic sounds. For
example, in the word _matsya_,
_ma_ is followed by a conjunct
of _ta_, _sa_, and _ya_ to make
_tsya_ . In the word _kya_, _ka_ and
_ya_ combine to form the _kya_
conjunct.


lava Devanagari
_Parimal Parmar, 2019_


**SHIROREKHA**


Among the North Indian scripts,
four share close structural
similarities: Devanagari, Bangla,
Gurmukhi, and Gujarati. The
first three feature the shirorekha
or shiro line, the horizontal
headstroke from which letters
hang. The letters are further
constructed on vertical bars of
full and half lengths. Gujarati
notably lacks the shirorekha but
retains the rest of the structural
qualities of the other three.


Anek Devanagari
Anek Bangla
Anek Gurmukhi
Anek Gujarati
_Ek Type, 2021_

INDIC TYPOGRAPHY


**TEXTURE**


The most challenging aspect of designing typefaces with
Indic and Latin counterparts is matching the textures of body
text. Because Devanagari characters are more complex than
Latin, they are designed to be lighter, creating more white
space. Kannada and Telugu characters have a horizontal
contrast (while Latin letters have vertical contrast), and they
have multiple levels of matras below the baseline. Large
multiscript type families aim to match disparate scripts
without homogenizing them.

Kigelia:
A Typeface
for Africa

WRITTEN BY
MARK JAMRA AND
NEIL PATEL


+ Saki Mafundikwa, _Afrikan_
_Alphabets: The Story of Writing_
_in Afrika_ (West New York, NJ:
Mark Batty Publisher, 2004).



The Kigelia type family was designed by Mark Jamra and Neil
Patel, working with experts inside and outside the scripts’ language communities. Named after the _Kigelia africana_ tree, which
grows across tropical Africa, Kigelia supports N’ko, ADLaM, Vai,
Ge’ez, Tifinagh, Osmanya, Arabic, Latin, Cyrillic, and Greek.
More than two thousand languages are spoken in Africa by
approximately 1.2 billion people. Some countries host more than
one hundred languages, each one attached to a unique ethnic
group. African languages and scripts spread to South America
and the Caribbean via the global slave trade. [+] Today, national
identities in Africa are remnants of colonialism and the division
of the continent by European powers. National boundaries cut
through entire populations of ethnic groups. As a result, many
Africans identify more strongly with their ethnic group than with
their nationality. Many governments retained vestigial colonial
languages to support business transactions and avoid showing
favoritism to specific ethnic groups. Although French, English,
and Portuguese are lingua franca in many regions, many people
prefer their native language for day-to-day communication.
Kigelia is designed with mobile devices in mind. These devices
have superseded desktop computing as the motor of communications and commerce in Africa. However, despite the growing
penetration of pay-as-you-go phones, many users don’t access
the Internet because of the lack of content in African languages
and scripts. Broader language support and culturally respectful
typefaces can help this content flourish.
The value of language and script support goes beyond day-today communication and content creation. Over the past decade,
African entrepreneurs and developers have created innovative
mobile-based products ranging from payment services and agricultural management tools to blockchain-based property management tools. These homegrown innovations rely on smartphone technology. Eliminating communication barriers across
the continent is helping these ideas become the robust backbone
of Africa’s burgeoning high-tech industry.



200 MULTIPLICITY OF SCRIPTS


**ADLAM**


writing system: Alphabet
Reading direction: Right to left
Variants: Connected and unconnected
Languages: Fulfulde, Pular
Speakers: 50 million


ADLaM is the youngest writing system in
Kigelia. It is used for Pular, the language
of the Fulani people of West Africa.
Created in the 1980s by Abdoulaye and
Ibrahima Barry of Guinea, ADLaM has
rapidly spread as a vehicle of literacy
through a large region. In Senegal,
instruction in ADLaM has been authorized
for general education in public schools.


**GE’EZ**


writing system: Abugida (alphasyllabary)
Reading direction: Left to right
Languages: Amharic, Tigrinya, Gamo,
Gurage, Tigre, Sebatbeit, Harari, Basketo,
Gumuz
Speakers: 65 million


Ge’ez is more than two thousand years old.
It originated as an abjad, an alphabet using
only consonants. The abugida developed by
adding vocalic diacritics to the consonantal
letters, resulting in the present-day script.
An adaptation to several modern languages
of Eritrea and Ethiopia was made possible
by the addition of language-specific letters.


thinking with type, third edition 201


KIGELIA: A TYPEFACE FOR AFRICA


**N’KO**


writing system: Alphabet
Reading direction: Right to left
Variants: Connected and unconnected
Language: Manding in the dialects Maninka,
Dioula, Bambara, and Mandinka
Speakers: 40 million


N’ko was invented by Souleymane Kanté of
Guinea and introduced in 1949 as a viable
alternative to the Latin and Arabic scripts,
which were sometimes used to transcribe
Manding. These scripts were insufficient
because Manding is a tonal language. Thus
N’ko was conceived with marks for both
vowels and tonal properties.


**OSMANYA**


writing system: Alphabet
Reading direction: Left to right
Language: Somali
Speakers: 16.6 million


Osmanya is a Somali writing system that is
no longer in daily use but is still valuable
for recording and restoring to digital form
manuscripts and various original artifacts
that are important to Somali culture and
history. It was invented between 1920 and
1922 by Osman Yusuf Kenadid to transcribe
the Somali language.


202 MULTIPLICITY OF SCRIPTS


**TIFINAGH**


writing system: Abjad (traditional
consonant-only script), alphabet (modern
Neo-Tifinagh)
Reading direction: Left to right or right to
left, depending on community
Languages: Amazigh (Tamazight), Tuareg
Speakers: 20 million


Tifinagh, the writing system of the Berber
and nomadic Tuareg peoples of northern
Africa and the Sahara region, is around
two thousand years old. Neo-Tifinagh is a
modern, twentieth-century adaptation that
introduced vowels into the system. NeoTifinagh is currently taught in some Moroccan
schools and used for Berber language
publications.


**VAI**


writing system: Syllabary
Reading direction: Left to right
Language: Vai
Speakers: 119,500


The Vai syllabary, created in 1830, is
the writing system of the Vai people
in Liberia and Sierra Leone. Kigelia
includes three hundred Vai glyphs.

drawings to demonstrate modern design
principles. The edges of the Illustrations and
ornaments align with blocks of text to show
a strong feeling of vertical axis. Sprunger’s
drawings appear in Douglas C. McMurtrie’s
