204 SCAFFOLDS AND SKELETONS


scaffolds and
skeletons


A layout is an arrangement of elements. The word _layout_ was applied to printing in the early twentieth century; it is still used
today to describe the abstract scaffolding of a composition.
Guiding the eye and establishing emphasis, layouts organize
posters, publications, and digital platforms. Over time, designers have used principles such as balance, alignment, hierarchy,
white space, framing, and grids to express different purposes and
points of view.
Visual balance is a metaphor that equates shapes and lines with
concrete objects. Why do some compositions seem lopsided,
rickety, or stuck in the mud? We instinctively connect graphic arrangements with our experience of how objects respond to
gravity and natural forces. A centered or justified column of text
is like a tree rooted to the ground, while ragged texts are more
like clouds or coastlines. Symmetry appears throughout nature,
from a flake of snow to a human skeleton. Nature loves asymmetry, too. Corals, crystals, and sand dunes grow and change in
irregular patterns.
Every layout reflects symmetry, asymmetry, or both. Asymmetrical typography took flight in the early twentieth century,
overturning centuries of tradition. Magazines and newspapers
bustled with diverse content, and modern art opened people’s
minds to abstraction. Modern designers dismissed symmetry as
a relic of the past, and they embraced the dynamism and efficiency of irregular layouts and rhythmic grids.


thinking with type, third edition 205


**FRAMING**


+ Jacques Derrida, _The_
_Truth in Painting_, trans.
Geoff Bennington and
Ian McLeod (Chicago:
University of Chicago Press,
1987), 61.
++ Owen Jones, _The_
_Grammar of Ornament_
(London: Studio Editions,
1986), first published
1856; Vanessa Zúñiga
Tinizaray, _Aproximación_
_a un Vocabulario Visual_
_Básico Andino: Análisis_
_Morfológico y Semiótico_
(Editorial Académica
Española, 2012); Thomas
Weill, _New Grammar of_
_Ornament_ (Zurich: Lars
Müller Publishers, 2021).



In traditional books, pages mirror each other across the central
spine. This physical spine reinforces the symmetry of two-page
layouts, affecting how we see, hold, and interact with content.
Classical margins create a pristine barrier around a solid text
block. Page numbers, running heads, commentary, and notes
often appear in the margins, providing an interface for books
and countering the singularity of the text block.
In addition to the classical norm of the single-column page,
multicolumn layouts appear in type history, from Gutenberg’s
Bible to complex layouts based on medieval manuscripts. On the
pages of Anton Koberger’s Latin Bible (1497), scholarly commentary surrounds scripture passages. Polyglot (multilingual)
books break the surface into different blocks to display several languages simultaneously. Such formats present multiple
streams of text within the sovereignty of the frame.
Philosopher Jacques Derrida has explained how frames in
Western art separate the work from daily life. A frame, pedestal,
or border elevates art, removing it from the realm of the ordinary. Derrida wrote, “The frame…disappears, buries itself, effaces
itself, melts away at the moment it deploys its greatest energy. The
frame is in no way a background…but neither is its thickness as margin a figure. Or at least it is a figure that comes away of its own accord.” [+] Frames focus the eye, raising the status of the work while
diminishing their own presence. Ornaments are often designed
to amplify structure. [++] Jewelry circles the neck, hips, and wrists.
Carved moldings trace windows and doorways and create transitions between ceilings and walls. Frames and ornaments echo
core motifs, dividing spaces and filling them up.
Typography is an art of framing. Often playing a supporting
role to content, typography recedes to the edges of attention.
Margins and white space oscillate between present and absent,
visible and invisible. Drop shadows, underlines, and starbursts
attract the eye and separate words from busy backgrounds. Ornament is a visual surplus that can distract from and emphasize
content. Frames are at once invisible and necessary.

thinking with type, third edition 209


**DIVIDING SPACE**


+ Paul Stirton, _Jan Tschichold_
_and the New Typography:_
_Graphic Design Between the_
_Wars_ (New York: Bard Graduate
Center, 2019).



Modern designers and architects in the 1920s rejected the central axis of classical books and buildings. Fighting against rigid
frames and static structures, designers revised everything from
city plans to kitchen cabinets. Modern life was messy. An ad
might include headlines, running text, photographs, illustrations, logos, and ornaments. Asymmetrical layouts were built to
withstand this clattering, clanky content stream.
Jan Tschichold’s book _The New Typography_, published in 1928,
translated ideas from Futurism, Dada, Constructivism, De Stijl,
and the Bauhaus into practical advice for commercial printers
and working designers. Whereas Futurism and Dada attacked
the social order, Tschichold promoted modern design as an efficient tool for business. The “new typography” spread quickly from the avant-garde to the mainstream. [+] The principles of
layout shifted from padding content with protective margins to
dividing space with dots, lines, dynamic axes, and typography
itself. A small, dark logo could pin down an airy text block, and
invisible guidelines could hold bits and pieces in tension.


Jan Tschichold, diagram from _The New_
_Typography_, 1928 (redrawn). Tschichold warned
against forcing text to wrap around blocks moored
at the center of the page (left). Instead, images
should be staggered in relation to content (right).
Tschichold wrote that these redesigned pages
would be even more effective if the photo­graphic
halftones (called “blocks”) were produced in fixed
rather than arbitrary sizes.



210 SCAFFOLDS AND SKELETONS


++ David Jury, _Graphic Design_
_Before Graphic Designers:_
_The Printer as Designer and_
_Craftsman_ (London: Thames &
Hudson, 2012).


William Addison Dwiggins,
_Layout in Advertising_
(Harper and Brothers, 1928).
Dwiggins is credited with
introducing the term _graphic_
_design_ . His drawing of iron
weights (above left) shows
that “a heavy weight near
the fulcrum balances a light
weight out on the end of the
beam.” His layout drawings
(above right) use a vertical
or diagonal axis to anchor
elements.


+++ William Addison Dwiggins,
_Layout in Advertising_ (New York:
Harper and Brothers, 1928),
68–69.



In the United States, printers used layout drawings to guide
the design process from the initial concept to the printed piece.
Such drawings were created by so-called “layout men.” Working
in printshops, ad agencies, and publishing houses, these skilled
artisans helped found the modern practice of graphic design. [++]

Twentieth-century layout drawings were both practical and
theoretical. Practically, they provided instructions for setting
type and assembling a final lockup for letterpress printing. Theoretically, they mapped the optical pathways and visual tensions
of a design. Layout drawings by William Addison Dwiggins use
flush-left text to create organic headings that counterbalance
and energize his centered compositions.


White space is crucial to the art of layout. Before the rise of
modern design, empty space in advertising was an unthinkable
waste of money. Modern designers, however, cherished white
space as a pause for the overburdened eye and a luxurious mark
of quality and sophistication. Visual balance and tension helped
white space feel structured and intentional, not empty.
Dwiggins celebrated the interplay between white space and
printed areas: “Here a peninsula of letterpress reaches out into the
paper ocean, there an inlet of ‘white space’ invades the land. This
give and take between text and paper is the game that the designer
directs to produce his characteristic and conspicuous silhouette.”
Like natural landscapes, language and typography are irregular;
thus the “bays and promontories need to be emphatic changes.” [+++]
Dwiggins preferred an organic mix of long and short lines over
smooth edges and perfect shapes.



thinking with type, third edition 211


**GRID AS**
**PROGRAM**


Designing Programmes
(Verlag Arthur Niggli, 1964).
Written and designed by Karl
Gerstner. Reproduced with
kind permission, Letterform
Archive.


+ Emil Ruder, _Typography_ (New
York: Hastings House, 1981), 14.
++ Karl Gerstner, “Designing
Programmes,” in _Graphic_
_Design Theory: Readings from_
_the Field_ (New York: Princeton
Architectural Press, 2009), 58.
+++ Lorraine Wild, “Notes on
Practice: Expanded, Compacted,
Exploded,” in _After the Bauhaus_
_and Before the Internet: A_
_History of Graphic Design_
_Pedagogy,_ ed. Geoff Kaplan (New
York: no place press, 2022),
316–31.



After World War II, graphic designers in Switzerland honed
avant-garde ideas into a practical methodology. Karl Gerstner,
Josef Müller-Brockmann, Emil Ruder, and others developed
an austere new style that hoped to clear the ground for a more
democratic society. They embraced sans-serif typefaces, lowercase letters, flush-left text, and a powerful new layout tool: the
grid. Ruder wrote, “The mechanical production of printing types
and composition within a right-angled system…makes a clear structure and cleanly ordered relationships imperative.” [+] The spreads
of a book or the walls of an exhibition became continuous, open
fields structured by an invisible net of columns and rows.
Max Bill applied typographic grids to book design as early as
1940. He used narrow margins—not much bigger than the gap
between columns—to emphasize the grid’s outward expansion.
Cropping images to fit the modules of the grid yields rectangles
with unusual proportions. Variation and surprise emerge from
the constraints of the system.
Gerstner’s book _Designing Programmes_ (1964) is a manifesto for
systems-oriented design. Inspired by the new field of computer
programming, he defined rules and variables for generating multiple solutions: “To describe the problem is part of the solution….
The more exact and complete these criteria are, the more creative
the work becomes. The creative process is to be reduced to an act
of selection.” [++] Like a responsive web design, Gerstner’s identity
for Boîte à Musique (Music Box), a record shop in Basel, Switzerland, expands and contracts as needed.
The systematic approach known as Swiss design attracted
followers worldwide. Books, magazines, and university courses spread this new way of working, whose transparent methods
are intentionally teachable. [+++] Some designers thought Swiss
design was irrelevant to the needs of consumer society or local
communities; grids and Helvetica were products of a privileged,
cleaned-up world. Many designers, however, welcomed the opportunity to make data and information easier to organize and
understand. Train schedules and pill bottles could be simplified

permission, Letterform Archive.

GRID AS
PROGRAM


Neue Plakat Kunst (New
Poster Art), 1959. Poster
designed by Manfred Durner.
Published in _Typographische_
_Monatsblätter_ .


+ Max Bill, “on typography,”
_Schweizer Graphische_
_Mitteilungen_, no. 4, 1946;
reprinted in Gerd Fleischmann,
Hans Rudolf Bosshard, and
Christoph Bignens, _Max Bill:_
_Typography_ (Salenstein: Niggli,
1999).
++ For a broad cultural history of
the grid, see Hannah B. Higgins,
_The Grid Boo_ k (Cambridge:
MIT Press, 2009). On recent
principles, see Ulysses Voelker,
_Structuring Design: Graphic_
_Grids in Theory and Practice_
(Salenstein: Niggli, 2020).



and reordered, achieving unprecedented clarity. Grids proved
especially useful for corporations, which produced hefty standards manuals to educate the designers and consultants responsible for producing products, packages, and signage.
In addition to constructing uniform grids, Swiss designers used the forms and rhythms of text itself to create invisible
guidelines. Manfred Durner’s promotion for _Neue Plakat Kunst_
(New Poster Art) uses the three words of the headline to divide
space and align content. Bill wrote that typography organized
this way “gains in simple spatial excitement and quiet self-same
clarity.” [+] The type creates its own grid.


Grids reflect the underlying framework of typography, from
the physical blocks used in letterpress printing to the rulers,
guidelines, and x/y coordinates employed in graphics software. Avant-garde designers in the 1910s and ’20s exposed the
mechanical grid of letterpress, pushing it to the surface. PostWWII designers built a total design methodology around the
grid. They hoped to support an egalitarian social order by wiping
away centered hierarchies.
Grids have evolved across centuries of typographic development. They are instruments designed with intention, and they
are an inescapable filter for countless systems of writing and reproduction. Designers today use grids to create layouts for print,
screen, and the built environment. Grids can be simple or complex, repetitive or irregular. Grids respond to content (text, image, data) and modes of display (page, screen, window, wall). A
grid is a resilient skeleton tied to the muscular mass of content. [++]

in 1953 by Inge Aicher-Scholl, Otl Aicher, and
Max Bill. The school built on Bauhaus ideas in
a systematic manner.



This issue of the _ulm_ journal, designed by

thinking with type, third edition 215


**UNIVERSAL**
**LAYOUT**


+ William Gibson,
_Neuromancer_ (New York:
Ace, 2020), 68. First
published in 1984.


++ Legacy Russell, _Glitch_
_Feminism: A Manifesto_
(London: Verso, 2020), 11.


+++ Kat Holmes, _Mismatch:_
_How Inclusion Shapes_
_Design_ (Cambridge, MA: MIT
Press, 2018.



William Gibson’s 1984 science fiction novel, _Neuromancer,_ envisioned cyberspace as a vast, ethereal grid. Case, the data cowboy at the center of Gibson’s tale, leaves behind the “meat” of his
body and drifts off into a “transparent 3D chessboard extending to
infinity.” [+] Gibson envisioned cyberspace as an infinite grid that
was hands-free and device-independent. This boundless, disembodied matrix lit up an internal surface of his mind.
Networked digital media soon became ubiquitous—but not
disembodied. Growing up online in the 1990s, Legacy Russell
used digital tools to craft her own identity (and produce her own
body) across numerous iterations. She writes, “We want a new
framework, we want new skin.…Through the digital, the body ‘in
glitch’ finds its genesis.” [++] Glitch feminism seeks to free human
bodies from the restrictive norms of race and gender.
The modern Internet is littered with another kind of body:
an appalling clutter of devices. These obstinate objects demand
their own interfaces, power sources, and pixel dimensions. Designers create dynamic layouts and fluid typography for this glut
of gadgets. Unmoored by strict grids, responsive web layouts react to viewports, component sizes, and user preferences. Images,
logos, type blocks, and icons change size and position in relation
to each other and to the boundaries of the screen.
The web’s transcendent promise is to provide access to everyone, everywhere. Language translation software and localization
protocols help companies change the design and content of websites to reflect the languages and customs of different regions.
The accessibility tools baked into smartphones verbalize and describe words, pictures, and the physical environment, expanding
the reach of countless bodies. [+++] Every website should be accessible for people with low vision, no vision, or motor impairments.
Achieving these goals requires describing images with alt text
(alternative text) and designing layouts that can be read in linear
sequences. Universal design demands creativity and care. Upholding ideals of transparency and openness helps information
find its way to everybody.

Eugene De Lopatecki was a
designer and educator who
taught at the Cooper Union in
New York in the 1930s. Here,
de Lopatecki uses an implied
diagonal line (right) to energize
static content (left). The design
remains primarily symmetrical.


To organize his layouts, de
Lopatecki used an “internal
skeleton of lines at right
angles.” The skeleton can be
perpendicular or oblique to
the edges of the page.


De Lopatecki and other
designers in the 1920s and ’30s
tried to make jumbled elements
work together. Diagonal lines
spanning the layout help create
order.


Diagram from Eugene de

thinking with type, third edition 219


Symmetry and
Asymmetry



Symmetrical layouts reflect the balance of the human body.
These layouts often feel stable and straightforward. Important
items are easy to find: right in the middle.
Asymmetrical layouts require trickier balancing acts. They
often feel active, charged with energy, and a little less obvious.
Aligning typography with other elements on the page or with
the guidelines of a grid can help hold the composition together.


**SYMMETRY** **ASYMMETRY**



Borders and margins function like
picture frames, defining a barrier
between the inside and outside
of the layout. The frame holds the
composition together.


220 BALANCE AND ALIGNMENT



Grids are built from horizontal and
vertical lines. The grid determines
the size and position of elements.
The grid anchors the content from
the inside out.


**SYMMETRY** **ASYMMETRY**


Symmetry and asymmetry can work
together. These digital covers for the
Indian magazine _Akademi_, designed
by Aarman Roy in 2021, use diverse
compositions to create visual impact.


thinking with type, third edition 221


Grouping In basic geometry, a point has zero dimensions. Add one dimension to create a line, add height to create a plane, and add depth
to create a three-dimensional space. In a layout, a single letter
is like a point. Rows of letters are lines, and columns are planes.
Gestalt psychology shows how perception organizes visual
stimuli into meaningful patterns. Grouping implies relation
+ Lukas Oppermann, “The ships between elements by building larger sets. [+] This process of
Gestalt Principle of Proximity—in Action,” December 29, 2020, sorting and organizing helps humans and other creatures make
UX Collective, uxdesign.cc/ sense of the world. We are able to see a tree as a single object
how-to-enhance-your-designwith-the-gestalt-principles-of- without being distracted by the millions of leaves and thousands
proximity-a7828452058b. of branches it contains, yet we can instantly shift focus and see

the smaller parts. Likewise, when people read a text, they perceive individual letterforms (points) and the larger groups they
form (lines, planes, patterns, and relationships).


**POINT** **LINE** **PLANE**


222 BALANCE AND ALIGNMENT


**POINT AND LINE TO PLANE**


A tiny word behaves like a point,
punctuating space. As the words
grow larger, the individual characters
become more prominent. The process
of reading shuffles between separate
glyphs and the larger objects they form
(words, lines, and planes).


Weight and line spacing affect the
unity of the text block. Heavier type
and bigger line spaces emphasize
individual letters and lines, while
lighter text and tighter line spaces
create an even texture and coherent
text block.

thinking with type, third edition 223


GROUPING Proximity is the distance between elements. Proximity helps establish relationships among subheads, labels, captions, images,
and other content. Similarity and alignment also help establish
groups. Breaking the dominant pattern of the group attracts the
eye and creates emphasis. Ungroup an element by changing its
color, weight, or size.


**PROXIMITY**


♥ clear groups × muddled groups


Vertical spacing helps
readers understand the
structure of a document.



♥ Clear groups | Proximity creates ×

relationships. The distance
between the headline is bigger than
the distance between paragraphs.


224 BALANCE AND ALIGNMENT



Muddled groups | This layout is
confusing because there is more space
between the paragraphs than between
the headline and the text.


**SIMILARITY**


Color, shape, orientation, and value
establish groups. Use these cues to
emphasize or downplay content.


**ALIGNMENT**


Aligning elements along an axis
creates groups. In Gestalt psychology,
this is called “common fate.” The eye
and brain link elements that appear to
follow a shared path.





♥ Clear groups | Proximity and similarity organize the containers into groups. Color ×

unifies the headlines. Alignment connects
the main images and captions.



Muddled groups | Uniform spacing causes
the groups to merge together, making the
layout cluttered and confusing. Illustration by
Lukas Oppermann.

Aligning
Elements



Graphic designers spend enormous amounts of energy lining
stuff up. A picture and a caption can align across the top, bottom,
or either side. Pictures, frames, text blocks, user interface components, marginalia, and other components can all be aligned to
build connections and create the appearance of order.


Aligning text | Letterforms have irregular
shapes, so aligning elements isn’t obvious.
Horizontal alignments include cap height to
cap height, x-height to x-height, and baseline
to baseline (below). Vertically, two texts can
align along a column edge or along features
of prominent letterforms. Aligning a caption
or column to an image helps connect them
visually (right).

thinking with type, third edition 227


Borders


Frames inside of frames
Traditional designs for books
and advertisements often
feature multiple borders,
boxes, and frames.



Rules and ornaments are stocked in letterpress printshops
alongside letters and spacing material. Borders can be combined
to build closed boxes and frames, or they can be used as open
horizontal or vertical lines.
Although boxes and frames are part of type history, they don’t
always look old-fashioned. Borders focus the eye and gather content into groups. Like grids, they can define large areas or break
up space into smaller chunks. Borders and boxes can be direct
and playfully obvious—like frames from comic books, supermarket flyers, and old newspaper ads. Layer, overlap, and divide
framed areas to imply motion and depth.



Buttoned up | Lowercase letters
inside a box must be centered
by eye because of the ascenders
and descenders.


228 BALANCE AND ALIGNMENT



Luka Lenie (above) created _The Perfect_
_School,_ a self-published zine, to propose
alternatives to traditional education. Her
layouts break up content with emphatic
boxes (which sometimes misbehave).
Durf te denken means “dare to think.”

Anna Kulachek (opposite) designed
a series of posters for Strelka Institute
that borrow borders and boxes from pop
culture: comic books, supermarket ads,
movie marquees, and digital interfaces.

230


Das Bauhaus in Dessau, 1924. Herbert Bayer
was a student and teacher at the Bauhaus. His
Bauhaus letterheads are manifestos for a new
typographic order. Lines of type divide the
surface into functional zones. Dashes indicate
axes for folding the sheet and positioning
text. Bayer believed that all letters should



be lowercase, a point expounded in small
print across the bottom of the letterhead.
He claimed that uppercase letters were
wasteful and unnecessary. Reproduced with
kind permission, Letterform Archive. © 2023
Artists Rights Society (ARS), New York / VG
Bild-Kunst, Bonn.



thinking with type, third edition 231


Manuscript
Grid


**DESIGNING SPREADS**


These diagrams show different
ways to arrange facing pages
in relation to the central spine
of a book. Most manuscript
grids are symmetrical, but
they can also be asymmetrical,
as shown in the fourth scheme.
Based on diagrams by Philippe
Schuwer, _Typographische_
_Monatsblätter_, 1959.



The simplest grid is a single column of text surrounded by margins. Called a manuscript grid or a text grid, this format appears
in classic novels and many nonfiction books.
When software prompts you to set a document’s page size,
margins, and columns, the program calculates a grid and generates guidelines automatically. Alternatively, you can design a
custom grid from the inside out by setting the margins to zero
and then positioning guidelines on a blank page. Then apply
these custom guidelines to a parent page.
Books and magazines function as spreads (facing pages). The
two-page spread, rather than the individual page, is the main
unit of print publications. Left and right margins become inside
and outside margins. Margins can differ from top to bottom and
from inside to outside.











232 GRIDS


classical manuscript grid | Book
structures built from the proportions of
the page have been used since the era of
medieval manuscripts. Begin by drawing
diagonals across the spread. Next, draw a
perpendicular line from point A to the top
of the page (point B). Connect B to C to
determine the top of the text box.



Designers and printers have created many
variations of this design approach. Such
methods use the shape of the page—
rather than units of measure—to create
the layout structure. The result is generous
and pleasing. Designed by Heather Hansen
in a course taught by Barbara Glauber.



thinking with type, third edition 233


Column Grid



Most publications employ a column grid. Gutenberg’s Bible
has two columns. Magazines and newspapers have eight, nine,
twelve, or more columns. A block of text can span several columns or just one. Create variety by making blocks that occupy
different numbers of columns.
Unlike borders and boxes, grid lines lie dormant until they are
brought to life by text, headlines, images, and small bits like page
numbers and running heads. Content reveals the grid. Horizontal hang lines break up the vertical pattern. The gaps between
columns (called gutters) hold areas of white space.


three-column grid | Irregularly sized
images and captions break the rhythm of
this three-column grid. _The US-Mexico_
_Border: Place, Imagination, and Possibility_
(Craft Contemporary, 2018). Catalog
designed by ELLA, coordinated by Sasha
Ali. Exhibition curated by Lowery Stokes
Sims and Ana Elena Mallet.



234 GRIDS


**HANG LINE**


Horizontal divisions create another level of
order in a multicolumn grid. For example,
body text can be dropped significantly below
the headline. Multiple columns with different
lengths can hang from a horizontal tether.
In architecture, a horizontal reference line
is called a datum. The tops of windows and
doors might share a datum.


Five-column grid | This publication uses a fivecolumn grid. Horizontal hang lines add structure.
Designed by Clemens Schedler, Büro für konkrete
Gestaltung, for Hotel Therme, Switzerland, 2003.


thinking with type, third edition 235


Modular Grid



A modular grid has horizontal rows and vertical columns. The
modules govern the size, shape, and placement of text blocks and
pictures. In the 1950s and ’60s, Swiss graphic designers, including Gerstner, Ruder, and Müller-Brockmann, devised modular
grid systems like those shown here.
The height of the module is determined by the measure of
the text from baseline to baseline. This rectangle is rarely a perfect square. Designing such a grid requires careful planning.
Implementing the design requires patience (and tolerance for
rule-breaking when needed).


Emil Ruder worked and taught in
Switzerland in the 1950s and ’60s.
His classic textbook is simply named
_Typography_ (Arthur Niggli Verlag,
1967). Ruder’s book uses a modular
grid for its own construction. The
layout shown here demonstrates a
nine-square modular grid. Photograph:
Dan Meyers.



236 GRIDS


Karl Gerstner created this elaborate grid.
It consists of six vertical columns and six
horizontal modules, overlaid by grids of
two, three, four, and six columns and rows.
Vertically, the grid is governed by a 10-pt
measure, which determines the spacing of
type from baseline to baseline. Redrawn
from _Designing Programmes_ (Arthur Niggli
Verlag, 1964).

thinking with type, third edition 239


Baseline
~~Grid~~


The headline and
body text above share
a baseline, but this
caption goes its own
way. Only the first line
of the caption aligns
with the baseline of
the body text.


**HORIZONTAL UNITS**



A baseline grid governs the vertical rhythm of an entire docu~~ment. Baseline grids help align columns and text blocks horizon-~~
tally, which is useful for multicolumn publications. To make the
different parts of a hierarchy conform to the baseline grid, the
body text, headings, and captions must use related line spacing.
To create a baseline grid, choose the type size and leading of
your text, such as 10-pt Signifier with 12 pts leading (10/12).
Avoid auto-leading so that you can work with whole numbers
that divide evenly. Use this increment to set the baseline grid in
your document preferences.
To style headlines, captions, and other elements, choose line
spacing that works with the baseline grid, such as 18/24 for headlines, 14/18 for subheads, and 8/12 for captions. Some headings
or captions may hit the grid every third or fourth line. In CSS,
choose similar increments for line-height to approximate the
unity of a baseline grid.



To create rows for a modular grid,
count how many lines fit in a full
column of text. Choose a number that
divides evenly into the line count. A
column with forty-two lines of text
divides neatly into seven rows with six
lines each. Reserve one line in each
group for the gutter (with no gutter
after the last line). Adjust the top and/
or bottom margins to absorb leftovers.

Designers who worship the supremacy
of the baseline grid derive comfort from
knowing that a unifed mathematical
order has locked every element in place.
Baseline grids require the line height or
leading of every paragraph to use numbers with common denominators.


~~ricoleta |~~ ~~_Jorge Cisterna_~~


measure for measure | This layout uses a
baseline grid of 12, established by the body
text (9/12). The largest heading meets the
baseline every third line. The microcopy
(7/8) lands on the baseline every fourth line.
The sidebar is set 12/12, which is very tight,
but it always matches its neighbor.



Skeptics doubt whether designers or
readers reap much beneft from the
restrictions dictated by baseline grids.
Some lines may look too widely spaced,
while other lines might feel too close
together. Some text blocks will only
“catch up” after a few lines. [1]

grid, align the first line of two columns
and let the rest fall organically. This
allows you to craft each heading style to
its best advantage. This approach was
used to design this book.

Digital content lives on desktops, phones, tablets, watches, and
more. Responsive layouts react to the viewport (available screen
space). Media queries track the size of the viewport or the size of
individual components and trigger changes to the layout.
In one approach to responsive design, different layout styles
kick in when the width of the viewport passes a given size threshold. More dynamic solutions (known as intrinsic web design or
fluid typography) allow typographic settings to change gradually as the user drags a screen into a taller or wider shape. This
approach helps retain proportions and avoid nasty transitions
between viewport sizes. As a viewport or container changes size,
features such as type size, optical type styles, variable type settings, line-height, and color can all change. [+]

desktop, tablet, phone | The
layouts and typography of this digital
magazine change across viewport
sizes. _Bustle_ (2022), designed by Code
and Theory/Bustle Digital Group.


thinking with type, third edition 243


Serial Design


Piet Zwart designed this
series of advertisements
in the 1920s. He used the

to construct unusual
letterforms and dynamic
compositions. Zwart created
dozens of advertisements
and product catalogs for

cables. Each one is unique
yet expresses a common
brand identity. Reproduced
with kind permission,
Letterform Archive. © 2023
Artists Rights Society (ARS),
New York / c/o Pictoright
Amsterdam.



Serial design is the process of applying typographic hierarchies
across multiple pages, publications, posters, media posts, or interface components. Consistent typography unifies the identity
of a brand or the voice of an author or organization. If the system
is flexible and robust, each item in the series can stand out. Serial
design approaches are used in web design, publication design,
signage, packaging, and more.
Websites and digital products are built from modular components. Each component contains the DNA of the overall system.
For example, UI cards appear in product catalogs, event listings,
and recipe platforms. Following a consistent template, the layout
and typography of each card help users find content and make
selections. UI cards can be rearranged and resized in response to
different viewports (such as desktop or mobile screens) or different content (such as longer or shorter entries). A card design can
become a building block or prototype applied to larger projects.

ideas for the look and feel
of the product and brand.
These designs for interface
cards include dark and light
themes.


from cell to organism

which consist of distinctive

intermediate


Soyeon Kwon



Grids are not the only way to arrange content. To begin this
exercise, arrange text with a modular grid, using a few sizes of
black text in a single type family. Next, try alternative layout
structures. You can discover ideas by exploring nature, cultural
heritage, found objects, commercial vernaculars, game boards,
architecture, digital media, generative algorithms, or artificial
intelligence. Apply one or more of these approaches to your text.
The examples shown here use an avant-garde manifesto as a
template for creating personal content. F. T. Marinetti launched
the Italian Futurist movement by publishing a manifesto in
1909. His bombastic text declared war on museums, libraries,
feminism, and conventional typography. You can satirize Marinetti’s florid battle cry by plugging in your own words and values.

thinking with type, third edition 247


**exercise**
Extended
Series


advanced



Brand identities, websites, publications, and ad campaigns are
examples of serial design. These large-scale endeavors display
varied content with a unified voice. In a website, a single chunk of
content may appear in multiple formats and viewports. To create
your own serial design system, collect content for a set of posters
or social media posts. Use this typographic system as the DNA
for a website or publication. This project explores hierarchy,
grids, grouping, responsive design, and other design concepts.

prateek bisht
Poster and website


248 GRIDS


**1. COLLECT CONTENT**


First, gather content related
to any compelling subject,
such as films, pets, cheese,
or notable biographies.
Organize your data in a table.
Include a range of shorter
and longer texts. Short texts
provide “crunchy bits” that
will bring texture and detail
to your project.


**2. DESIGN A SERIES**


Next, use your structured
collection of content to
design a series of posters
or social media posts. Each
item in the series should be
distinctive yet connected to
the larger group.

contributors


Yeohyun Ahn is a typographer,
interaction designer, and educator. Her
work has appeared in the _Washington_
_Post_, _New York Times Magazine_, and
_Letter Arts Review_ . She teaches at
the University of Wisconsin–Madison.
Her computational art explores social
homelessness, addressing marginalization in professional areas of American
society. She holds an MFA from MICA
(Maryland Institute College of Art).


Khajag Apelian is a lettering artist,
type designer, and graphic designer.
He grew up between Dubai and Beirut
and was raised in an Armenian family.
He has developed typefaces in Arabic,
Armenian, and Latin. He operates
under the name Debakir (Armenian for
“printed type”) and has worked with
IBM, Apple, Samsung, Twitter, HP, and
Disney ME. He teaches design at the
American University of Beirut.


Akshita Chandra is an Indian graphic
designer based in Baltimore. She
experiments with themes of multiplicity
(of emotions, cultures, forms, and
physicality). She has been recognized
by Adobe, Type Directors Club (TDC),
Communication Arts, Society of
Typographic Arts, _PRINT_, The Design
Kids, Femme Type, and _AIGA Eye on_
_Design_ . She holds an MFA from MICA.


Mark Jamra is a type designer and
retired professor of graphic design
at Maine College of Art and Design
in Portland. He is the proprietor of
TypeCulture, an independent digital
type foundry, and cofounder of Jamra
Patel. His lettering and typefaces have
been shown in numerous exhibitions
and have received awards from the
TDC and Association Typographique
Internationale (ATypI).


Moon Jang is a graphic designer, artist,
and educator. She holds an MFA in
graphic design from RISD (Rhode Island
School of Design). She is an associate
professor at Lamar Dodd School of Art,
the University of Georgia. Her work


250



has appeared in exhibitions including
365: AIGA Annual Design (New York),
Graphic Arts Festival of Chaumont
(France), Golden Bee (Russia), and
The Fourth Typographic Biennale:
Typojanchi 2015 (Korea).


Caspar Lam and YuJune Park are
partners at Synoptic Office, based in
New York and Hong Kong. Caspar is
the undergraduate program director
and an assistant professor of communication design at Parsons School of
Design. YuJune is an assistant professor
of communication design at Parsons.
Caspar and YuJune lecture widely and
have served on the boards of AIGA
New York and the TDC.


Taekyeom Lee is an educator,
multi-disciplinary designer, and maker.
He uses unconventional materials and
digital fabrication to create tangible
typography, graphics, and objects. His
artwork has gone viral on social media.
He has presented his work at national
and international conferences and in
various publications, and he delivers
workshops and lectures worldwide.


Ellen Lupton is a designer, writer,
and educator. Her books include _Design_
_Is Storytelling_, _Graphic Design Thinking_,
and _Extra Bold: A Feminist, Inclusive,_
_Anti-Racist, Nonbinary Field Guide_
_for Graphic Designers_ . She is the Betty
Cooke and William O. Steinmetz Design
Chair at MICA. She is Curator Emerita
at Cooper Hewitt, Smithsonian Design
Museum in New York City, where her
exhibitions included _Herbert Bayer:_
_Bauhaus Master_ and _The Senses: Design_
_Beyond Vision_ .


Wael Morcos is a graphic designer
and type designer from Lebanon and a
partner at Morcos Key in New York City.
He received a BA in graphic design from
Notre Dame University (Lebanon) and
an MFA from RISD. He has developed
Arabic-Latin bilingual typefaces and
graphic identities, and he works in print
and exhibition design. He was named



a Young Gun by the Art Directors Club
and an Ascender by the TDC.


Shiva Nallaperumal is a graphic
designer, type designer, and art director
from India. In 2018, he co-founded
November with Juhi Vishnani; their
clients include Apple Music, Twitter,
Uber, NBA, Indian Institute of
Architects, Kobe Bryant (Granity
Studios), and the _New York Times_ .
Shiva has worked with Commercial
Type, Typotheque, Bold Monday, Sharp
Type, and Lost Type Co-op. He holds
an MFA in graphic design from MICA,
where he was a designer-in-residence
2021–22. He is a member of Alliance
Graphique Internationalem (AGI).


Ryoko Nishizuka studied type design
at Musashino Art University. She
is a principal designer of Japanese
typography at Adobe. She is the
designer of Kazuraki, the world’s first
fully proportional OpenType Japanese
font. Other projects include the
pan-CJK typeface family Source Han
Sans and Source Han Serif and Higumin,
based on the handwriting of painter
Yuko Higuchi.


Neil Patel entered the field of type
design after working as a photolithography process engineer in the
semiconductor industry. Using his
strength in analytical problem-solving
and system-level thinking, Neil has
developed custom typefaces for clients
such as IDEXX Laboratories, NCR, and
Cartoon Network. He is the designer
behind Tetradtype, an independent
digital type foundry, and cofounder
of Jamra Patel.
